I have setup ssh authentication keys in github.
However when I do  'git push --set-upstream claude-expense-tracker main' git is asking me for user id and password

Apr 18
This usually means Git is using the HTTPS remote URL instead of SSH. Let's check and fix it.

1. Check your current remote URL:

bash
git remote -v
If it shows something like https://github.com/... that's the problem. SSH remotes look like git@github.com:...

2. Fix it by switching to the SSH URL:

bash
git remote set-url claude-expense-tracker git@github.com:YOUR_USERNAME/YOUR_REPO.git
Replace YOUR_USERNAME and YOUR_REPO with your actual GitHub username and repo name.

3. Verify it changed:

bash
git remote -v
Should now show git@github.com:...

4. Then push again:

bash
git push --set-upstream claude-expense-tracker main
Also worth checking — if the remote name is claude-expense-tracker but it's actually pointing to a different repo than intended, you can confirm the correct SSH URL by going to your repo on GitHub → green Code button → SSH tab → copy that URL.

git push --set-upstream claude-expense-tracker main git@github.com:madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git
error: src refspec git@github.com does not match any
error: failed to push some refs to 'https://github.com/madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git'

Apr 18
You mixed the command up — you accidentally included the SSH URL as part of the git push command instead of setting it as the remote URL first.

Run these two commands separately:

Step 1 — Fix the remote URL:

bash
git remote set-url claude-expense-tracker git@github.com:madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git
Step 2 — Then push:

bash
git push --set-upstream claude-expense-tracker main
how to estimate token use

Apr 18
Here are the main ways to estimate token usage for the Anthropic API:

Quick rule of thumb:

~1 token ≈ 4 characters (or ~¾ of a word) in English
"Hello, how are you?" ≈ 5 tokens
1,000 words ≈ ~1,300 tokens
1. Use Anthropic's Tokenizer Tool Visit: https://lunary.ai/anthropic-tokenizer or check the Claude docs for the official tokenizer.

2. Count tokens via the API response Every API response includes token counts in the usage field:

json
{
  "usage": {
    "input_tokens": 25,
    "output_tokens": 142
  }
}
3. Count tokens before sending (in code) Use Anthropic's token counting endpoint:

javascript
const response = await fetch("https://api.anthropic.com/v1/messages/count_tokens", {
  method: "POST",
  headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
  body: JSON.stringify({
    model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
    messages: [{ role: "user", content: "Your prompt here" }]
  })
});
const { input_tokens } = await response.json();
4. Rough cost estimation For Claude Sonnet 4, approximate pricing:

Input: ~$3 per million tokens
Output: ~$15 per million tokens
So a typical back-and-forth (500 input + 500 output tokens) ≈ $0.009 (less than a cent).

Practical tips to reduce token use:

Keep system prompts concise
Don't repeat context unnecessarily
Set a reasonable max_tokens limit on responses
Use claude-haiku for simpler tasks (much cheaper)
In claude code when genrating a new application, how to calculate estimated number of tokens

Apr 18
When generating a new application with Claude Code, here's how to think about token estimation:

What consumes tokens in Claude Code:

Your instructions/prompts
All files Claude reads (it reads existing files before editing)
All code it generates (output)
Back-and-forth conversation history
Rough estimates for app generation:

Task	Approx Tokens
Simple script (50 lines)	2k–5k
Small app (5–10 files)	20k–50k
Medium app (20–30 files)	80k–200k
Large app with tests/docs	300k–500k+
Practical ways to estimate before starting:

1. Count your source files manually

bash
# See total characters in your project
find . -name "*.js" -o -name "*.ts" -o -name "*.py" | xargs wc -c
# Divide total characters by 4 = rough token count
2. Check usage after a session Claude Code shows token usage and cost at the end of each session in the terminal.

3. Use /cost command during a session Type /cost in Claude Code to see current session token usage and cost at any point.

Tips to reduce token usage in Claude Code:

Be specific in your prompt — vague prompts lead to back-and-forth
Use .claudeignore to exclude node_modules, build folders, large files
Break large apps into focused sessions (auth first, then UI, etc.)
Avoid asking Claude to re-read files it already processed
The biggest cost driver is usually how many files Claude has to read to understand context, not just what it writes.

Are you trying to track token usage for your expense tracker app?

yes

Apr 18
For tracking token usage in your expense tracker app, here are some practical approaches:

1. Track it in your own code (most reliable) Log token usage from every API response — it's in every response automatically:

javascript
const response = await anthropic.messages.create({...});

console.log({
  input_tokens: response.usage.input_tokens,
  output_tokens: response.usage.output_tokens,
  cost: (response.usage.input_tokens * 3 / 1_000_000) + 
        (response.usage.output_tokens * 15 / 1_000_000)
});
2. Store it per user/session Save token counts to your database so you can:

See cost per user
Set usage limits
Generate your own usage reports
3. Set a spending limit in the Console Go to console.anthropic.com/settings/billing and set a monthly spend cap so you don't get surprised by a big bill while testing.

4. Use the Anthropic usage API

GET https://api.anthropic.com/v1/usage
This lets you pull usage data programmatically.

Since you're building an expense tracker, it would actually be a neat feature to track the AI cost per request as a line item — gives you full visibility into what the app costs to run per user.

I know python. Is it a good idea to learn node.js

Apr 18
Given that you're already building with Claude's API and working on a JavaScript-based expense tracker, yes — it's worth learning, but with some context:

Reasons to learn Node.js:

Your expense tracker is likely already using JavaScript/Node.js on the backend, so it's immediately practical
The Claude SDK and most AI tooling has excellent Node.js support
Huge ecosystem (npm) and very common in full-stack web apps
If you know Python, the learning curve is manageable — the concepts are the same, just different syntax
Where Python still wins:

Data science, ML, scripting, automation
Cleaner syntax for beginners
Better libraries for data analysis (pandas, numpy)
Honest take:

If you're building web apps and APIs → Node.js is worth it
If you're doing data/AI/scripting work → stick with Python
If you're doing both → knowing both is very common and practical
The transition is easier than you think since you already know programming concepts. The main adjustments are:

Asynchronous programming (async/await — Python has this too)
Callbacks and Promises
JavaScript quirks (this, == vs ===)
Since you're already working with Claude Code and building apps, Node.js will complement your Python knowledge well rather than replace it.

how to call function greet

Apr 19
In Node.js, you call a function the same way as Python — just use the function name with parentheses:

javascript
// Define it
function greet(name) {
  return `Hello ${name}`
}

// Call it
greet("Alice")                    // returns "Hello Alice" but doesn't print
console.log(greet("Alice"))       // prints: Hello Alice

// With a variable
const name = "Madhav"
console.log(greet(name))          // prints: Hello Madhav
Same works for arrow functions:

javascript
const greet = (name) => `Hello ${name}`

console.log(greet("Madhav"))      // prints: Hello Madhav
Python comparison:

python
# Python
def greet(name):
    return f"Hello {name}"

print(greet("Madhav"))   # same idea, different syntax
The key difference from Python is just:

Use console.log() instead of print()
Wrap the function call inside console.log() if you want to see the output
SyntaxError: Malformed arrow function parameter list

Apr 19
This error usually means a syntax mistake in how you wrote the arrow function. Common causes:

1. Missing parentheses around parameters

javascript
// WRONG
const greet = name, age => `Hello ${name}`

// CORRECT
const greet = (name, age) => `Hello ${name}`
2. Typo or extra character

javascript
// WRONG
const greet = (name) => `Hello ${name}`
                      ^^ make sure this is =>  not just =

// WRONG
const greet = (name) = > `Hello ${name}`  // space between = and >

// CORRECT
const greet = (name) => `Hello ${name}`
3. Using backtick vs quote wrongly

javascript
// WRONG - mixing quotes
const greet = (name) => "Hello ${name}"   // ${name} won't work in double quotes

// CORRECT - must use backticks for template literals
const greet = (name) => `Hello ${name}`
Paste your code here and I'll spot the exact issue. The error message is very specific so it's usually just a small typo.

Also — what editor are you using? VS Code will underline these errors before you even run the file, which saves a lot of time.

    try{
        const response = await fetch(url)
        const data =await response.json()
        return data
    } catch(error){
        console.error("something went wrong : ", error)
    }

Show more
Apr 19
Spot the typo — aysnc should be async, and the syntax for async arrow functions is slightly different:

javascript
// WRONG
const getData = aysnc(url) => {   // ❌ typo: aysnc, and wrong placement

// CORRECT
const getData = async (url) => {  // ✅ async goes before the parenthesis
Here is your fixed code:

javascript
const getData = async (url) => {
    try {
        const response = await fetch(url)
        const data = await response.json()
        return data
    } catch (error) {
        console.error("something went wrong : ", error)
    }
}
Two fixes:

aysnc → async (typo)
async(url) => → async (url) => (async goes before the parentheses, not wrapping them)
Easy way to remember the pattern:

javascript
const myFunction = async (param) => {
    const result = await somethingAsync()
    return result
}
For above function  I got 

Promise { <pending> }
something went wrong :  SyntaxError: Unexpected token '<', "<!doctype "... is not valid JSON
    at JSON.parse (<anonymous>)

console.log(getData("http://google.com"))
Apr 19
Two separate issues here:

Problem 1: Promise { <pending> }

You forgot await when calling the function. getData is async, so you must await it:

javascript
// WRONG
console.log(getData("http://google.com"))

// CORRECT
const result = await getData("http://google.com")
console.log(result)
But await only works inside an async function, so wrap it:

javascript
const main = async () => {
    const result = await getData("http://google.com")
    console.log(result)
}

main()
Problem 2: Unexpected token '<' error

Google.com returns an HTML page (which starts with <!doctype html>), not JSON. So response.json() fails because it's trying to parse HTML as JSON.

getData is meant for APIs that return JSON — not websites. Try a real JSON API instead:

javascript
const main = async () => {
    const result = await getData("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/todos/1")
    console.log(result)
}

main()
This will print something like:

json
{ "userId": 1, "id": 1, "title": "delectus aut autem", "completed": false }
Summary:

Problem	Cause	Fix
Promise <pending>	Missing await when calling async function	Wrap in async main() and await the call
Unexpected token '<'	URL returns HTML, not JSON	Use a JSON API URL
Help me write an async Node.js function called callClaude(prompt) that calls the Anthropic API and returns the text response, with proper error handling.

Apr 19
Here's a complete working example:

Step 1 — Set up your project

bash
mkdir claude-test && cd claude-test
npm init -y
npm install @anthropic-ai/sdk
Add this to your package.json:

json
"type": "module"
Step 2 — Create index.js

javascript
import Anthropic from '@anthropic-ai/sdk'

const client = new Anthropic({
    apiKey: "your-api-key-here"  // replace with your key from console.anthropic.com
})

const callClaude = async (prompt) => {
    try {
        const response = await client.messages.create({
            model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
            max_tokens: 1000,
            messages: [
                { role: "user", content: prompt }
            ]
        })

        // extract the text from the response
        const text = response.content[0].text

        // log token usage so you can track cost
        console.log("Tokens used:", response.usage)

        return text

    } catch (error) {
        console.error("Claude API error:", error.message)
        throw error
    }
}

// main function to run it
const main = async () => {
    const reply = await callClaude("What is 2 + 2? Answer in one sentence.")
    console.log("Claude says:", reply)
}

main()
Step 3 — Run it

bash
node index.js
You should see:

Tokens used: { input_tokens: 20, output_tokens: 12 }
Claude says: 2 + 2 equals 4.
Better practice — use environment variable for API key

Never hardcode your API key. Instead:

bash
# Mac/Linux
export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY="your-key-here"

# Windows
set ANTHROPIC_API_KEY="your-key-here"
Then in your code:

javascript
const client = new Anthropic()  // automatically reads ANTHROPIC_API_KEY from environment
Summary of the pattern:

javascript
const callClaude = async (prompt) => {
    try {
        const response = await client.messages.create({...})
        return response.content[0].text   // always how you get the text out
    } catch (error) {
        console.error(error.message)
        throw error
    }
}
Once this works, you're ready to plug Claude into your expense tracker. Want to do that next?

javascript
const callClaudeJSON = async (prompt) => {

        return JSON.parse(text)
    } catch {

Here's exactly where to add it in package.json:

json
{
  "name": "claude-test",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "description": "",
  "main": "index.js",
  "type": "module",
  "scripts": {
    "test": "echo \"Error: no test specified\" && exit 1"
  },
  "keywords": [],
  "author": "",
  "license": "ISC",
  "dependencies": {
    "@anthropic-ai/sdk": "^0.39.0"
  }
}
Just add "type": "module" as a top level line — anywhere inside the outer {} works, but conventionally it goes after "main".

But since it's already working without it — don't touch it! You likely have one of two things going on:

Your file is named index.mjs (the .mjs extension tells Node it's a module automatically)
You're using require() style imports instead of import — which works without "type": "module"
You only need "type": "module" if you want to use:

javascript
import Anthropic from '@anthropic-ai/sdk'   // modern style - needs type:module
vs the older style which works without it:

javascript
const Anthropic = require('@anthropic-ai/sdk')  // old style - works by default
Which import style are you using in your index.js?

import Anthropic from '@anthropic-ai/sdk'

Apr 20
Then you do need "type": "module" — but since it's already working, Node.js must be accepting it anyway in your version.

To be safe, add it now so it doesn't break later:

Step 1 — Open package.json in your project folder. You can open it in VS Code or any text editor.

Step 2 — Find this line:

json
"main": "index.js",
Step 3 — Add "type": "module" right after it:

json
"main": "index.js",
"type": "module",
Step 4 — Save the file and run node index.js again — should still work exactly the same.

Quick way to open it from terminal:

bash
# VS Code
code package.json

# or just print it to check
cat package.json
Paste the output of cat package.json here if you want me to show you exactly where to insert it in your specific file.

It has the following : 

"type": "commonjs",
Apr 20
That's why it works — commonjs is the default older style, but your import statement is modern ES module syntax. They conflict, so change it:

Just change commonjs to module:

json
"type": "module",
That's the only change needed. Then save and run node index.js again — it should still work fine.

Quick explanation of the difference:

CommonJS	ES Module
package.json	"type": "commonjs"	"type": "module"
Import style	const x = require('...')	import x from '...'
Export style	module.exports = x	export default x
Since you're already using import, just make sure package.json says "type": "module" and everything is consistent.

Great, let's do Step 5 — Modules & npm!

The concept — instead of putting all code in one file, you split it into logical files and import what you need. Just like Python's import.

Create this folder structure:

claude-test/
├── index.js
├── package.json
└── utils/
    └── claude.js
utils/claude.js — put all Claude-related code here:

javascript
import Anthropic from '@anthropic-ai/sdk'

const client = new Anthropic()

export const callClaude = async (prompt) => {
    try {
        const response = await client.messages.create({
            model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
            max_tokens: 1000,
            messages: [{ role: "user", content: prompt }]
        })
        return response.content[0].text
    } catch (error) {
        console.error("Claude API error:", error.message)
        throw error
    }
}

export const callClaudeJSON = async (prompt) => {
    const text = await callClaude(prompt + "\nRespond with valid JSON only, no extra text.")
    try {
        return JSON.parse(text)
    } catch {
        console.error("Not valid JSON:", text)
        return null
    }
}

    try {
        const response = await client.messages.create({
            model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
            max_tokens: 1000,

    }

Apr 27
Step 6 — Working with Objects & Arrays

These are used constantly in Node.js — especially when handling Claude API responses and expense data.

Destructuring — pulling values out cleanly

javascript
// Without destructuring (verbose)
const response = { total: 381, insight: "Accommodation is biggest expense" }
const total = response.total
const insight = response.insight

// With destructuring (clean)
const { total, insight } = response
console.log(total)    // 381
console.log(insight)  // "Accommodation is biggest expense"
Works great with Claude API responses:

javascript
const { total, category_breakdown, insight } = await summarizeExpenses(expenses)
console.log(total)
console.log(insight)
Array destructuring

javascript
const [first, second, ...rest] = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
console.log(first)   // 10
console.log(second)  // 20
console.log(rest)    // [30, 40, 50]
Spread operator — merging objects

javascript
const baseRequest = { model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514", max_tokens: 1000 }
const myRequest = { ...baseRequest, max_tokens: 500 }  // overrides max_tokens
console.log(myRequest)
// { model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514", max_tokens: 500 }
Array methods — the big 3

These replace most for loops in modern Node.js:

javascript
const expenses = [
    { description: "Taxi", amount: 45, category: "Transport" },
    { description: "Lunch", amount: 67, category: "Meals" },
    { description: "Hotel", amount: 210, category: "Accommodation" },
    { description: "Uber", amount: 24, category: "Transport" }
]

// map() — transform each item (like Python list comprehension)
const amounts = expenses.map(e => e.amount)
console.log(amounts)  // [45, 67, 210, 24]

// filter() — keep only matching items
const transport = expenses.filter(e => e.category === "Transport")
console.log(transport)  // [{Taxi...}, {Uber...}]

// reduce() — combine into one value
const total = expenses.reduce((sum, e) => sum + e.amount, 0)
console.log(total)  // 346
Python comparison:

Python	Node.js
[e['amount'] for e in expenses]	expenses.map(e => e.amount)
[e for e in expenses if e['category'] == 'Transport']	expenses.filter(e => e.category === 'Transport')
sum(e['amount'] for e in expenses)	expenses.reduce((sum, e) => sum + e.amount, 0)
Putting it all together — add this to utils/expenses.js:

javascript
export const getTotal = (expenses) => 
    expenses.reduce((sum, e) => sum + e.amount, 0)

export const filterByCategory = (expenses, category) =>
    expenses.filter(e => e.category === category)

export const getAmounts = (expenses) =>
    expenses.map(e => e.amount)

export const getExpenseSummary = (expenses) => {
    const { description, amount, ...rest } = expenses[0]  // destructuring example
    return {
        total: getTotal(expenses),
        count: expenses.length,
        average: Math.round(getTotal(expenses) / expenses.length),
        largest: Math.max(...getAmounts(expenses))  // spread into Math.max
    }
}
Test it in index.js:

javascript
import { getTotal, filterByCategory, getExpenseSummary } from './utils/expenses.js'

const expenses = [
    { description: "Taxi", amount: 45, category: "Transport" },
    { description: "Lunch", amount: 67, category: "Meals" },
    { description: "Hotel", amount: 210, category: "Accommodation" },
    { description: "Uber", amount: 24, category: "Transport" }
]

console.log("Total:", getTotal(expenses))
console.log("Transport only:", filterByCategory(expenses, "Transport"))
console.log("Summary:", getExpenseSummary(expenses))
Expected output:

Total: 346
Transport only: [ { description: 'Taxi', amount: 45, category: 'Transport' },
                  { description: 'Uber', amount: 24, category: 'Transport' } ]
Summary: { total: 346, count: 4, average: 87, largest: 210 }

what does sse stand for

May 13
SSE = Server-Sent Events

It's a way for a server to push data to the client continuously over a single HTTP connection — instead of the client having to keep asking.

Normal API call (request/response):

Client:  "Hey, give me the response"
Server:  "Here is the complete response"  ← one big chunk at the end
SSE (streaming):

Client:  "Hey, start sending me the response"
Server:  "Here..."
Server:  "is..."
Server:  "the..."
Server:  "response..."        ← words arrive one by one
Server:  [done]
Why it matters for Claude:

This is how Claude streams responses word by word — instead of waiting for the full response, you see it typing in real time, just like on claude.ai.

javascript
// Non-streaming (waits for complete response)
const response = await client.messages.create({
    model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
    max_tokens: 1000,
    messages: [{ role: "user", content: prompt }]
})
console.log(response.content[0].text)  // prints all at once

// Streaming (SSE - prints word by word)
const stream = await client.messages.stream({
    model: "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
    max_tokens: 1000,
    messages: [{ role: "user", content: prompt }]
})

for await (const chunk of stream) {
    if (chunk.type === "content_block_delta") {
        process.stdout.write(chunk.delta.text)  // prints as it arrives
    }
}
You also saw SSE earlier when setting up MCP servers — the URL https://mcp.asana.com/sse means that MCP server uses SSE to stream tool results back to Claude in real time.

Want me to show you how to add streaming to your callClaude function?

what does fr. stand for

May 13
fr = fractional unit

It's a CSS unit used in grid layouts to divide available space into equal fractions.

css
grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr 1fr   /* 3 equal columns */
grid-template-columns: 1fr 2fr       /* col 2 is twice as wide as col 1 */
grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr       /* 2 equal columns */
Simple analogy: Think of fr like slicing a pizza:

1fr 1fr 1fr = 3 equal slices
1fr 2fr = one small slice, one large slice (double size)
In Node.js context — were you seeing it in CSS inside your expense tracker frontend, or somewhere else? Just want to make sure I'm explaining the right thing!

what does Fr. stand for in christianity

May 13
Fr. = Father

It's a title used for priests, mainly in:

Catholic church
Orthodox church
Anglican/Episcopal church
Examples:

Fr. John Smith — a Catholic priest named John Smith
You address them as "Good morning, Father" or "Father John"
Other common Christian title abbreviations:

Abbreviation	Meaning	Used for
Fr.	Father	Catholic/Orthodox priests
Rev.	Reverend	Protestant ministers
Br.	Brother	Male religious members (monks)
Sr.	Sister	Female religious members (nuns)
Bp.	Bishop	Senior church leaders
Abp.	Archbishop	Senior bishops
Mgr.	Monsignor	Senior Catholic priests
Pretty different from CSS fr units! 😄 Are you taking a break from coding?

In the meantime, common reasons an AWS policy fails to save even with valid JSON:

1. Copy-paste formatting issue Sometimes copying from a chat or document adds invisible characters. Try:

Click "Format JSON" button in the AWS console — it will reformat and highlight any issues
Or clear the editor completely and paste fresh
2. Wrong policy editor Make sure you're in the JSON tab not the Visual editor tab — sometimes switching between them corrupts the content

3. Policy size limit AWS has a 6,144 character limit per policy — not your issue here since this policy is tiny

4. Missing permissions to save Your AWS user might not have iam:PutUserPolicy or iam:AttachUserPolicy permission

how long does it take to propagate new secret key

May 14
In AWS Secrets Manager, propagation is usually near instant — but depends on where you're using it:

Secrets Manager itself:

New secret is available immediately after creation
GetSecretValue works within seconds
Where delays can happen:

Scenario	Delay
Reading secret in Lambda/EC2	Instant — fetched at runtime
IAM policy propagation	30 seconds to 2 minutes
Cached secret in your code	However long your cache TTL is
Cross-region replication	1–5 minutes
Most common reason it feels slow:

If you just attached the IAM policy that allows secretsmanager:GetSecretValue — the IAM policy propagation is usually what you're waiting for, not the secret itself. Give it 1-2 minutes then try again.

Quick test to check if it's working:

bash
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --secret-id YOUR_SECRET_NAME --region us-east-1
If that returns the value, the secret is ready and the issue is elsewhere in your code.

Are you fetching the secret in your Node.js expense tracker?

May 14
Simple fix — Weather_API_KEY is being used as a variable but it should be a string (in quotes):

python
# WRONG - Python thinks Weather_API_KEY is a variable
SecretId=Weather_API_KEY

# CORRECT - it's a string, needs quotes
SecretId="Weather_API_KEY"
Show me your get_secret() function and I'll give you the exact fix. It likely looks something like this:

python
def get_secret():
    client = boto3.client('secretsmanager')
    response = client.get_secret_value(
        SecretId=Weather_API_KEY        # ← missing quotes
    )
    return response['SecretString']
Fix it to:

python
def get_secret():
    client = boto3.client('secretsmanager')
    response = client.get_secret_value(
        SecretId="Weather_API_KEY"      # ← with quotes
    )
    return response['SecretString']
Also make sure "Weather_API_KEY" exactly matches the secret name in your AWS portal — including capital letters, underscores, and spacing. AWS secret names are case sensitive.

Please give me cli code to instanciate ec2 server

May 14
Here's the CLI code to launch an EC2 instance:

Minimal — quickest way to get started:

bash
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t2.micro \
  --count 1 \
  --region us-east-1
Practical — with key pair and security group so you can SSH in:

bash
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t2.micro \
  --key-name your-key-pair-name \
  --security-group-ids sg-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --count 1 \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=instance,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=my-server}]'
Before running — get these values:

1. Latest Amazon Linux AMI for your region:

bash
aws ec2 describe-images \
  --owners amazon \
  --filters "Name=name,Values=al2023-ami-*-x86_64" \
  --query 'sort_by(Images, &CreationDate)[-1].ImageId' \
  --output text \
  --region us-east-1
2. List your key pairs:

bash
aws ec2 describe-key-pairs --query 'KeyPairs[*].KeyName' --output table
3. List your security groups:

bash
aws ec2 describe-security-groups --query 'SecurityGroups[*].[GroupId,GroupName]' --output table
After launching — get your instance IP:

bash
aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=tag:Name,Values=my-server" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].[InstanceId,PublicIpAddress,State.Name]' \
  --output table
Then SSH in:

bash
ssh -i ~/.ssh/your-key-pair-name.pem ec2-user@YOUR_PUBLIC_IP
A few things to decide first:

Option	Recommendation
Instance type	t2.micro (free tier) or t3.small for Node.js apps
Region	Same region as your Secrets Manager secret
AMI	Amazon Linux 2023 is the latest standard
Key pair	Create one in AWS console first if you don't have one
Do you want to run your Node.js expense tracker on this EC2 instance?

Please add a tag to run setup script to the above code

May 14
Here's the updated command with a tag to run a setup script using EC2 User Data:

bash
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t2.micro \
  --key-name your-key-pair-name \
  --security-group-ids sg-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --count 1 \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --user-data file://setup.sh \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=instance,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=my-server},{Key=Setup,Value=true}]'
Create setup.sh in the same folder you run the command from:

bash
#!/bin/bash
# Update system
yum update -y

# Install Node.js
curl -fsSL https://rpm.nodesource.com/setup_20.x | bash -
yum install -y nodejs

# Install git
yum install -y git

# Clone your expense tracker repo
cd /home/ec2-user
git clone git@github.com:madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git
cd claude-expense-tracker

# Install dependencies
npm install

# Start the app
node index.js >> /var/log/app.log 2>&1
Check if setup script ran successfully after SSH-ing in:

bash
# View setup log
sudo cat /var/log/cloud-init-output.log

# View app log
sudo cat /var/log/app.log
Important notes:

--user-data file://setup.sh runs the script once on first boot as root
Script runs in the background — instance will show running before script finishes
Make sure setup.sh is in the same directory where you run the aws command
Replace your-key-pair-name and sg-xxxxxxxxxx with your actual values
Do you want me to add anything to the setup script — like fetching your Anthropic API key from Secrets Manager?

Where should I keep setup .sh on my mac min - folder?

May 14
Keep it in the same folder where you run the aws command — because the --user-data file://setup.sh path is relative to where you are in the terminal.

Since you're working in:

/Users/madhavathavale/workspace/MacMiniM2/AI-Code/
I'd suggest creating a dedicated folder:

bash
mkdir -p /Users/madhavathavale/workspace/MacMiniM2/AI-Code/aws-setup
cd /Users/madhavathavale/workspace/MacMiniM2/AI-Code/aws-setup
Then create setup.sh there:

bash
nano setup.sh
And run the aws ec2 run-instances command from that same folder.

Your folder structure would look like:

AI-Code/
├── aws-setup/
│   └── setup.sh          ← setup script here
├── aws-agentic-ai/       ← your existing Python code
└── claude-node-examples.js/
    └── claude-test/      ← your Node.js expense tracker
Quick tip — make sure setup.sh is executable before running:

bash
chmod +x setup.sh
Then run the aws ec2 run-instances command from inside aws-setup/ folder and the file://setup.sh reference will work correctly.

How to run jupyter notebook on this instance

May 14
Here's how to set up and access Jupyter Notebook on your EC2 instance:

Step 1 — SSH into your instance:

bash
ssh -i ~/.ssh/your-key-pair.pem ec2-user@YOUR_PUBLIC_IP
Step 2 — Install Jupyter on the EC2 instance:

bash
# Install Python pip
sudo yum install -y python3-pip

# Install Jupyter
pip3 install jupyter

# Install common data science packages
pip3 install pandas numpy matplotlib boto3
Step 3 — Configure Jupyter for remote access:

bash
# Generate config file
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password
jupyter notebook password
Step 4 — Start Jupyter on the instance:

bash
jupyter notebook \
  --no-browser \
  --port=8888 \
  --ip=0.0.0.0
Step 5 — Open security group port 8888:

bash
# Run this on your Mac
aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress \
  --group-id sg-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --protocol tcp \
  --port 8888 \
  --cidr 0.0.0.0/0 \
  --region us-east-1
Step 6 — Access from your Mac browser:

http://YOUR_PUBLIC_IP:8888
Better approach — use SSH tunneling (more secure):

Instead of opening port 8888 to the world, tunnel through SSH:

bash
# Run this on your Mac — creates a secure tunnel
ssh -i ~/.ssh/your-key-pair.pem \
    -L 8888:localhost:8888 \
    ec2-user@YOUR_PUBLIC_IP
Then on the EC2 instance start Jupyter:

bash
jupyter notebook --no-browser --port=8888
Then open on your Mac:

http://localhost:8888
This way port 8888 stays closed to the internet — much safer.

Add to your setup.sh to install automatically:

bash
# Install Jupyter
pip3 install jupyter pandas numpy matplotlib boto3

# Generate config
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set a default password (change 'mypassword' to something secure)
echo "c.NotebookApp.password = '$(python3 -c "from jupyter_server.auth import passwd; print(passwd('mypassword'))")'" >> ~/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py
Tip — use tmux so Jupyter keeps running after you disconnect:

bash
# Install tmux
sudo yum install -y tmux

# Start a tmux session
tmux new -s jupyter

# Start Jupyter inside tmux
jupyter notebook --no-browser --port=8888

# Detach from tmux (Jupyter keeps running)
# Press Ctrl+B then D

# Reattach later
tmux attach -t jupyter
Want me to add all of this to your setup.sh automatically?

May 14
Here's the complete setup.sh with everything included:

bash
#!/bin/bash

# ─── System Update ───────────────────────────────────────────
yum update -y

# ─── Install Node.js ─────────────────────────────────────────
curl -fsSL https://rpm.nodesource.com/setup_20.x | bash -
yum install -y nodejs

# ─── Install Git ─────────────────────────────────────────────
yum install -y git

# ─── Install Python & Jupyter ────────────────────────────────
yum install -y python3-pip tmux
pip3 install jupyter pandas numpy matplotlib boto3 anthropic

# ─── Configure Jupyter ───────────────────────────────────────
# Run as ec2-user (not root) so paths are correct
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'

# Generate Jupyter config
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password (change 'mypassword' to something secure)
python3 -c "
from jupyter_server.auth import passwd
pw = passwd('mypassword')
with open('/home/ec2-user/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py', 'a') as f:
    f.write(f\"c.NotebookApp.password = '{pw}'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.ip = '0.0.0.0'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.open_browser = False\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.port = 8888\n\")
"

# Create notebooks folder
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/notebooks

EOF

# ─── Clone Expense Tracker ───────────────────────────────────
cd /home/ec2-user
git clone https://github.com/madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git
cd claude-expense-tracker
npm install

# ─── Fetch Anthropic API Key from Secrets Manager ────────────
AWS_REGION="us-east-1"
SECRET=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "Weather_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text)
echo "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=$SECRET" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Start Jupyter in tmux session ───────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user tmux new-session -d -s jupyter \
  "jupyter notebook --notebook-dir=/home/ec2-user/notebooks"

# ─── Log completion ──────────────────────────────────────────
echo "Setup complete at $(date)" >> /var/log/setup-complete.log
Save it, then make it executable:

bash
chmod +x setup.sh
After instance launches, access Jupyter via SSH tunnel from your Mac:

bash

    -L 8888:localhost:8888 \

http://localhost:8888

Here's a clean setup.sh focused purely on Python and Jupyter:

bash
#!/bin/bash

# ─── System Update ───────────────────────────────────────────
yum update -y

# ─── Install Python & pip ────────────────────────────────────
yum install -y python3 python3-pip tmux git

# ─── Install Python packages ─────────────────────────────────
pip3 install \
  jupyter \
  pandas \
  numpy \
  matplotlib \
  seaborn \
  scikit-learn \
  boto3 \
  anthropic \
  requests \
  python-dotenv

# ─── Configure Jupyter ───────────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'

# Generate config
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password and config
python3 -c "
from jupyter_server.auth import passwd
pw = passwd('mypassword')
with open('/home/ec2-user/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py', 'a') as f:
    f.write(f\"c.NotebookApp.password = '{pw}'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.ip = 'localhost'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.open_browser = False\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.port = 8888\n\")
"

# Create folder structure
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/notebooks
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/data
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/scripts

EOF

# ─── Fetch API Keys from Secrets Manager ─────────────────────
AWS_REGION="us-east-1"

# Anthropic key
ANTHROPIC_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text)
echo "export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=$ANTHROPIC_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# OpenWeather key
WEATHER_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "Weather_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text)
echo "export WEATHER_API_KEY=$WEATHER_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Create a sample starter notebook ────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user python3 -c "
import json

notebook = {
    'nbformat': 4,
    'nbformat_minor': 5,
    'metadata': {
        'kernelspec': {
            'display_name': 'Python 3',
            'language': 'python',
            'name': 'python3'
        }
    },
    'cells': [
        {
            'cell_type': 'markdown',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': ['# Welcome to your EC2 Jupyter Notebook\n']
        },
        {
            'cell_type': 'code',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': [
                'import os\n',
                'import boto3\n',
                'import pandas as pd\n',
                'import numpy as np\n',
                'import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n',
                'print(\"All packages loaded!\")\n',
                'print(\"Anthropic key set:\", bool(os.environ.get(\"ANTHROPIC_API_KEY\")))'
            ],
            'outputs': [],
            'execution_count': None
        },
        {
            'cell_type': 'code',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': [
                '# Test Anthropic API\n',
                'import anthropic\n',
                'client = anthropic.Anthropic()\n',
                'response = client.messages.create(\n',
                '    model=\"claude-sonnet-4-20250514\",\n',
                '    max_tokens=100,\n',
                '    messages=[{\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Say hello in one sentence\"}]\n',
                ')\n',
                'print(response.content[0].text)'
            ],
            'outputs': [],
            'execution_count': None
        }
    ]
}

with open('/home/ec2-user/notebooks/starter.ipynb', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(notebook, f, indent=2)
"

# ─── Start Jupyter in tmux ────────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user tmux new-session -d -s jupyter \
  "jupyter notebook --notebook-dir=/home/ec2-user/notebooks"

# ─── Log completion ───────────────────────────────────────────
echo "Python setup complete at $(date)" >> /var/log/setup-complete.log

How will I get public IP

May 15
Several ways to get your EC2 instance's public IP:

1. AWS CLI (quickest):

bash
aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=tag:Name,Values=my-server" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].PublicIpAddress' \
  --output text \
  --region us-east-1
2. List all running instances with their IPs:

bash
aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].[InstanceId,PublicIpAddress,Tags[?Key==`Name`].Value|[0]]' \
  --output table \
  --region us-east-1
Output looks like:

-------------------------------------------------
|          DescribeInstances                    |
+---------------------+---------------+---------+
|  i-0abc123def456789 | 54.123.45.67  | my-server|
+---------------------+---------------+---------+
3. AWS Console (visual):

Go to console.aws.amazon.com
EC2 → Instances
Click your instance
Look for Public IPv4 address
4. From inside the instance (after SSH):

bash
curl ifconfig.me
Pro tip — save it to a variable:

bash
PUBLIC_IP=$(aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=tag:Name,Values=my-server" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].PublicIpAddress' \
  --output text \
  --region us-east-1)

echo $PUBLIC_IP

# Then SSH directly
ssh -i ~/.ssh/your-key-pair.pem ec2-user@$PUBLIC_IP
Note that the public IP changes every time you stop and start the instance. If you want a permanent IP, you need an Elastic IP — want me to show you how to set that up?

where are python packages installed

May 15
On your EC2 instance (Amazon Linux), Python packages installed with pip3 go here:

/usr/local/lib/python3.X/site-packages/
Check exact location:

bash
pip3 show anthropic
Output:

Name: anthropic
Version: 0.39.0
Location: /usr/local/lib/python3.11/site-packages
Other useful commands:

bash
# List all installed packages
pip3 list

# Check where pip installs by default
pip3 --version

# Check Python path
python3 -c "import sys; print(sys.path)"

# Find a specific package
python3 -c "import anthropic; print(anthropic.__file__)"
On your Mac Mini it's different:

Location	When
/usr/local/lib/python3.x/site-packages	System pip3
/Users/madhavathavale/Library/Python/3.x/lib/python/site-packages	User install (pip3 install --user)
/path/to/venv/lib/python3.x/site-packages	Virtual environment
Pro tip — use a virtual environment to keep packages isolated per project:

bash
# Create virtual environment
python3 -m venv /home/ec2-user/venv

# Activate it
source /home/ec2-user/venv/bin/activate

# Install packages into venv only
pip3 install -r requirements.txt

# Add to setup.sh
echo "source /home/ec2-user/venv/bin/activate" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
This keeps your project packages separate from system Python — much cleaner. Want me to update the setup.sh to use a virtual environment?

However I dont see packages such as pandas in the locations that you indicated

Show more
May 15
That's because the packages are being installed for the root user (since setup.sh runs as root), but you're looking in the ec2-user location.

Check root's package location:

bash
sudo pip3 show pandas
Or look here:

bash
ls /usr/local/lib/python3.*/site-packages/ | grep pandas
Quick way to find it:

bash
sudo python3 -c "import pandas; print(pandas.__file__)"
The problem — when you run Jupyter as ec2-user, it may not find packages installed by root.

Fix — install packages as ec2-user in setup.sh:

bash
# Instead of this (installs for root)
pip3 install -r requirements.txt

# Do this (installs for ec2-user)
sudo -u ec2-user pip3 install -r requirements.txt

# Or use the full path
sudo -u ec2-user /usr/bin/pip3 install -r requirements.txt
Quickest fix right now — reinstall as ec2-user:

bash
# SSH into your instance first
ssh -i ~/.ssh/your-key-pair.pem ec2-user@YOUR_PUBLIC_IP

# Then run
pip3 install pandas numpy matplotlib seaborn scikit-learn boto3 anthropic jupyter

# Verify
python3 -c "import pandas; print('pandas works!')"
This installs into ec2-user's home directory where Jupyter can find them. Want me to update the full setup.sh to install everything correctly as ec2-user?

May 15
Here's the fully updated setup.sh that installs everything correctly as ec2-user:

bash
#!/bin/bash

# ─── Log everything ──────────────────────────────────────────
exec > /var/log/setup.log 2>&1
echo "Setup started at $(date)"

# ─── System Update ───────────────────────────────────────────
yum update -y
yum install -y python3 python3-pip tmux git curl

# ─── Install Node.js ─────────────────────────────────────────
curl -fsSL https://rpm.nodesource.com/setup_20.x | bash -
yum install -y nodejs

# ─── Clone repo as ec2-user ──────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
cd /home/ec2-user
git clone https://github.com/madhav-athavale/claude-expense-tracker.git
EOF

# ─── Install Python packages as ec2-user ─────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
echo "Installing Python packages..."

pip3 install --user \
  jupyter \
  pandas \
  numpy \
  matplotlib \
  seaborn \
  scikit-learn \
  boto3 \
  anthropic \
  requests \
  python-dotenv

# Install from requirements.txt if it exists
if [ -f /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt ]; then
  pip3 install --user -r /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt
  echo "Installed from requirements.txt"
fi

echo "Python packages installed"
EOF

# ─── Install Node packages as ec2-user ───────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
cd /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker
npm install
echo "Node packages installed"
EOF

# ─── Configure Jupyter as ec2-user ───────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'

# Add local bin to PATH so jupyter command works
export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin

# Create folders
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/notebooks
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/data
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/scripts

# Generate Jupyter config
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password and settings
python3 -c "
from jupyter_server.auth import passwd
pw = passwd('mypassword')
with open('/home/ec2-user/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py', 'a') as f:
    f.write(f\"c.NotebookApp.password = '{pw}'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.ip = 'localhost'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.open_browser = False\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.port = 8888\n\")
"

echo "Jupyter configured"
EOF

# ─── Add PATH to ec2-user .bashrc ────────────────────────────
echo 'export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Fetch API keys from Secrets Manager ─────────────────────
AWS_REGION="us-east-1"

ANTHROPIC_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text 2>/dev/null)

if [ ! -z "$ANTHROPIC_KEY" ]; then
  echo "export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=$ANTHROPIC_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
  echo "Anthropic key fetched"
else
  echo "WARNING: Could not fetch Anthropic key"
fi

WEATHER_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "Weather_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text 2>/dev/null)

if [ ! -z "$WEATHER_KEY" ]; then
  echo "export WEATHER_API_KEY=$WEATHER_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
  echo "Weather key fetched"
else
  echo "WARNING: Could not fetch Weather key"
fi

# ─── Create starter notebook ──────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user python3 -c "
import json

notebook = {
    'nbformat': 4,
    'nbformat_minor': 5,
    'metadata': {
        'kernelspec': {
            'display_name': 'Python 3',
            'language': 'python',
            'name': 'python3'
        }
    },
    'cells': [
        {
            'cell_type': 'markdown',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': ['# Welcome to your EC2 Jupyter Notebook\n']
        },
        {
            'cell_type': 'code',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': [
                'import os\n',
                'import boto3\n',
                'import pandas as pd\n',
                'import numpy as np\n',
                'import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n',
                'print(\"All packages loaded!\")\n',
                'print(\"Anthropic key set:\", bool(os.environ.get(\"ANTHROPIC_API_KEY\")))\n',
                'print(\"Weather key set:\", bool(os.environ.get(\"WEATHER_API_KEY\")))'
            ],
            'outputs': [],
            'execution_count': None
        },
        {
            'cell_type': 'code',
            'metadata': {},
            'source': [
                '# Test Anthropic API\n',
                'import anthropic\n',
                'client = anthropic.Anthropic()\n',
                'response = client.messages.create(\n',
                '    model=\"claude-sonnet-4-20250514\",\n',
                '    max_tokens=100,\n',
                '    messages=[{\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Say hello in one sentence\"}]\n',
                ')\n',
                'print(response.content[0].text)'
            ],
            'outputs': [],
            'execution_count': None
        }
    ]
}

with open('/home/ec2-user/notebooks/starter.ipynb', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(notebook, f, indent=2)
print('Starter notebook created')
"

# ─── Start Jupyter in tmux as ec2-user ───────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin
tmux new-session -d -s jupyter \
  "jupyter notebook --notebook-dir=/home/ec2-user/notebooks"
echo "Jupyter started in tmux"
EOF

# ─── Verify installations ─────────────────────────────────────
echo "─── Verifying installations ───"
sudo -u ec2-user python3 -c "
import importlib
packages = ['pandas', 'numpy', 'matplotlib', 'seaborn', 'sklearn', 'boto3', 'anthropic']
for pkg in packages:
    try:
        importlib.import_module(pkg)
        print(f'  ✓ {pkg}')
    except ImportError:
        print(f'  ✗ {pkg} MISSING')
"

echo "─── Setup complete at $(date) ───"

I also want python version 3.13. The default is 3.9

Add this to your setup.sh right after the system update section:

bash
#!/bin/bash

# ─── Log everything ──────────────────────────────────────────
exec > /var/log/setup.log 2>&1
echo "Setup started at $(date)"

# ─── System Update ───────────────────────────────────────────
yum update -y
yum install -y tmux git curl gcc make openssl-devel bzip2-devel libffi-devel zlib-devel

# ─── Install Python 3.13 from source ─────────────────────────
echo "Installing Python 3.13..."
cd /tmp

# Download Python 3.13
curl -O https://www.python.org/ftp/python/3.13.0/Python-3.13.0.tgz
tar -xzf Python-3.13.0.tgz
cd Python-3.13.0

# Compile and install
./configure --enable-optimizations
make -j $(nproc)
make altinstall        # altinstall keeps python3.9 as default

# Verify
python3.13 --version
echo "Python 3.13 installed"

# ─── Make python3.13 the default ─────────────────────────────
alternatives --install /usr/bin/python3 python3 /usr/local/bin/python3.13 2
alternatives --set python3 /usr/local/bin/python3.13

# Link pip
ln -sf /usr/local/bin/pip3.13 /usr/local/bin/pip3

# Verify default is now 3.13
python3 --version
pip3 --version
echo "Python 3.13 set as default"

# ─── Install Python packages as ec2-user ─────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin

echo "Installing Python packages with Python 3.13..."
python3.13 -m pip install --user \
  jupyter \
  pandas \
  numpy \
  matplotlib \
  seaborn \
  scikit-learn \
  boto3 \
  anthropic \
  requests \
  python-dotenv

# Install from requirements.txt if it exists
if [ -f /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt ]; then
  python3.13 -m pip install --user \
    -r /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt
  echo "Installed from requirements.txt"
fi

echo "Python packages installed"
EOF

# ─── Add PATH to ec2-user .bashrc ────────────────────────────
echo 'export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
echo 'alias python3=python3.13' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
echo 'alias pip3="python3.13 -m pip"' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Configure Jupyter for Python 3.13 ───────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin

mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/notebooks
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/data
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/scripts

# Generate Jupyter config
python3.13 -m jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password and settings
python3.13 -c "
from jupyter_server.auth import passwd
pw = passwd('mypassword')
with open('/home/ec2-user/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py', 'a') as f:
    f.write(f\"c.NotebookApp.password = '{pw}'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.ip = 'localhost'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.open_browser = False\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.port = 8888\n\")
"

# Register Python 3.13 as a Jupyter kernel
python3.13 -m ipykernel install --user --name python3.13 --display-name "Python 3.13"

echo "Jupyter configured with Python 3.13"
EOF

# ─── Start Jupyter in tmux ────────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=$PATH:/home/ec2-user/.local/bin
tmux new-session -d -s jupyter \
  "python3.13 -m jupyter notebook --notebook-dir=/home/ec2-user/notebooks"
echo "Jupyter started"
EOF

# ─── Verify installations ─────────────────────────────────────
echo "─── Verifying installations ───"
python3.13 --version
sudo -u ec2-user python3.13 -c "
import importlib
packages = ['pandas', 'numpy', 'matplotlib', 'seaborn', 'sklearn', 'boto3', 'anthropic', 'jupyter']
for pkg in packages:
    try:
        importlib.import_module(pkg)
        print(f'  ✓ {pkg}')
    except ImportError:
        print(f'  ✗ {pkg} MISSING')
"

echo "─── Setup complete at $(date) ───"
Key things to note:

Detail	Explanation
make altinstall	Installs 3.13 without removing 3.9
alternatives --set	Makes 3.13 the default python3
python3.13 -m pip	Ensures pip uses 3.13 not 3.9
ipykernel install	Registers 3.13 as a kernel in Jupyter
Compile time	Takes 5-10 minutes — be patient
Warning — compiling Python takes time. Use at least a t3.small instance, not t2.micro, otherwise it will be very slow.

Yes! Install Miniconda — much faster than building from source, and you get the same python=3.13 syntax you're used to:

bash
#!/bin/bash

# ─── Log everything ──────────────────────────────────────────
exec > /var/log/setup.log 2>&1
echo "Setup started at $(date)"

# ─── System Update ───────────────────────────────────────────
yum update -y
yum install -y tmux git curl

# ─── Install Miniconda ───────────────────────────────────────
echo "Installing Miniconda..."
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
cd /home/ec2-user

# Download Miniconda
curl -O https://repo.anaconda.com/miniconda/Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh

# Install silently
bash Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh -b -p /home/ec2-user/miniconda3

# Remove installer
rm Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh

# Add conda to PATH
echo 'export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
echo 'source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh

# ─── Create Python 3.13 environment ──────────────────────────
echo "Creating Python 3.13 environment..."
conda create -n py313 python=3.13 -y

# ─── Activate and install packages ───────────────────────────
conda activate py313

pip install \
  jupyter \
  pandas \
  numpy \
  matplotlib \
  seaborn \
  scikit-learn \
  boto3 \
  anthropic \
  requests \
  python-dotenv

# Install from requirements.txt if exists
if [ -f /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt ]; then
  pip install -r /home/ec2-user/claude-expense-tracker/requirements.txt
fi

# Register as Jupyter kernel
python -m ipykernel install --user --name py313 --display-name "Python 3.13"

echo "Environment ready"
EOF

# ─── Make py313 the default conda env ────────────────────────
echo 'conda activate py313' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Configure Jupyter ───────────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh
conda activate py313

mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/notebooks
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/data
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/scripts

# Generate config
jupyter notebook --generate-config

# Set password and settings
python -c "
from jupyter_server.auth import passwd
pw = passwd('mypassword')
with open('/home/ec2-user/.jupyter/jupyter_notebook_config.py', 'a') as f:
    f.write(f\"c.NotebookApp.password = '{pw}'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.ip = 'localhost'\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.open_browser = False\n\")
    f.write(\"c.NotebookApp.port = 8888\n\")
"

echo "Jupyter configured"
EOF

# ─── Fetch API keys from Secrets Manager ─────────────────────
AWS_REGION="us-east-1"

ANTHROPIC_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text 2>/dev/null)

if [ ! -z "$ANTHROPIC_KEY" ]; then
  echo "export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=$ANTHROPIC_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
  echo "Anthropic key fetched"
else
  echo "WARNING: Could not fetch Anthropic key"
fi

WEATHER_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "Weather_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text 2>/dev/null)

if [ ! -z "$WEATHER_KEY" ]; then
  echo "export WEATHER_API_KEY=$WEATHER_KEY" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
  echo "Weather key fetched"
else
  echo "WARNING: Could not fetch Weather key"
fi

# ─── Start Jupyter in tmux ────────────────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh
conda activate py313

tmux new-session -d -s jupyter \
  "jupyter notebook --notebook-dir=/home/ec2-user/notebooks"
echo "Jupyter started in tmux"
EOF

# ─── Verify installations ─────────────────────────────────────
echo "─── Verifying installations ───"
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh
conda activate py313

python --version

python -c "
import importlib
packages = ['pandas', 'numpy', 'matplotlib', 'seaborn', 'sklearn', 'boto3', 'anthropic']
for pkg in packages:
    try:
        importlib.import_module(pkg)
        print(f'  ✓ {pkg}')
    except ImportError:
        print(f'  ✗ {pkg} MISSING')
"
EOF

echo "─── Setup complete at $(date) ───"

After I get the secret I I had to convert it to json to extract the actual api key. Is this the right approach? Is there a better way?

Show more
May 16
Yes, your approach is correct! AWS Secrets Manager always stores secrets as a JSON string, so json.loads() is the standard way to extract the value.

Why it's stored as JSON:

When you created the secret in the AWS console it stored it as:

json
{"Weather_API_KEY": "abc123youractualkey"}
So get_secret_value_response['SecretString'] returns that whole JSON string, and you need json.loads() to parse it.

Your code is fine, but here's a cleaner version:

python
import boto3
import json
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError

def get_secret(secret_name, key):
    """
    Generic secret fetcher
    secret_name: name of the secret in AWS
    key: the key inside the JSON to extract
    """
    session = boto3.session.Session()
    client = session.client(
        service_name='secretsmanager',
        region_name='us-east-1'
    )

    try:
        response = client.get_secret_value(SecretId=secret_name)
        return json.loads(response['SecretString'])[key]
    except ClientError as e:
        raise e
    except KeyError:
        raise KeyError(f"Key '{key}' not found in secret '{secret_name}'")

# Usage
weather_key = get_secret("Weather_API_KEY", "Weather_API_KEY")
anthropic_key = get_secret("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY", "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY")
Even better — cache it so you don't call AWS on every request:

python
import boto3
import json
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError
from functools import lru_cache

@lru_cache(maxsize=None)  # caches after first call
def get_secret(secret_name, key):
    session = boto3.session.Session()
    client = session.client(
        service_name='secretsmanager',
        region_name='us-east-1'
    )
    try:
        response = client.get_secret_value(SecretId=secret_name)
        return json.loads(response['SecretString'])[key]
    except ClientError as e:
        raise e

# First call hits AWS
weather_key = get_secret("Weather_API_KEY", "Weather_API_KEY")

# Second call returns cached value — no AWS call
weather_key = get_secret("Weather_API_KEY", "Weather_API_KEY")
Two alternative ways secrets are stored in AWS:

Storage type	How to retrieve
Key/value (your case)	json.loads(response['SecretString'])['your_key']
Plain text	response['SecretString'] directly — no json.loads needed
Binary	response['SecretBinary']
Pro tip — store all related keys in one secret to reduce AWS API calls and cost:

json
{
  "WEATHER_API_KEY": "abc123",
  "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY": "xyz789",
  "DB_PASSWORD": "secret"
}
Then fetch all at once:

python
def get_all_secrets(secret_name):
    session = boto3.session.Session()
    client = session.client(service_name='secretsmanager', region_name='us-east-1')
    response = client.get_secret_value(SecretId=secret_name)
    return json.loads(response['SecretString'])

secrets = get_all_secrets("my_app_secrets")
weather_key = secrets['WEATHER_API_KEY']
anthropic_key = secrets['ANTHROPIC_API_KEY']
One AWS call, all your keys. Much more efficient!

How to convert one of my functions n(e.g. get_secrets()) into a package and where should I keep this package?

May 16
Great question! Here's how to convert get_secret() into a reusable Python package:

Step 1 — Create this folder structure:

/Users/madhavathavale/workspace/MacMiniM2/AI-Code/
├── aws-agentic-ai/          ← your existing project
├── claude-expense-tracker/  ← your existing project
└── shared-libs/             ← new package folder
    └── aws_utils/
        ├── __init__.py
        ├── secrets.py
        └── README.md
Step 2 — Create secrets.py:

python
import boto3
import json
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError
from functools import lru_cache

@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def get_secret(secret_name, key=None, region_name='us-east-1'):
    """
    Fetch a secret from AWS Secrets Manager.
    
    Args:
        secret_name: name of the secret in AWS
        key: specific key to extract from JSON secret.
             If None, returns the full dict
        region_name: AWS region (default us-east-1)
    
    Returns:
        secret value as string, or full dict if key is None
    
    Example:
        get_secret("Weather_API_KEY", "Weather_API_KEY")
        get_secret("my_app_secrets")  # returns full dict
    """
    session = boto3.session.Session()
    client = session.client(
        service_name='secretsmanager',
        region_name=region_name
    )

    try:
        response = client.get_secret_value(SecretId=secret_name)
        secret = json.loads(response['SecretString'])
        
        if key is None:
            return secret          # return full dict
        return secret[key]         # return specific key

    except ClientError as e:
        raise e
    except KeyError:
        raise KeyError(f"Key '{key}' not found in secret '{secret_name}'")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        # secret is plain text, not JSON
        return response['SecretString']
Step 3 — Create __init__.py:

python
from .secrets import get_secret

__version__ = "1.0.0"
__all__ = ["get_secret"]
Step 4 — Create setup.py to make it installable:

python
from setuptools import setup, find_packages

setup(
    name="aws_utils",
    version="1.0.0",
    packages=find_packages(),
    install_requires=[
        "boto3",
    ],
    author="Madhav Athavale",
    description="Shared AWS utility functions"
)
Your final folder structure:

shared-libs/
└── aws_utils/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── secrets.py
    ├── setup.py
    └── README.md
Step 5 — Install it locally:

bash
cd /Users/madhavathavale/workspace/MacMiniM2/AI-Code/shared-libs
pip3 install -e .    # -e means editable — changes reflect immediately
Step 6 — Use it in any project:

python
# In aws-agentic-ai project
from aws_utils import get_secret

weather_key = get_secret("Weather_API_KEY", "Weather_API_KEY")

# In claude-expense-tracker project
from aws_utils import get_secret

anthropic_key = get_secret("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY", "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY")
Later — add more utilities to the package:

shared-libs/
└── aws_utils/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── secrets.py      ← get_secret()
    ├── s3.py           ← upload_file(), download_file()
    ├── dynamodb.py     ← get_item(), put_item()
    └── bedrock.py      ← call_bedrock()
Just add them to __init__.py:

python
from .secrets import get_secret
from .s3 import upload_file, download_file
from .dynamodb import get_item, put_item
Comparison of options for sharing code:

Option	Best for
pip install -e . (local)	Your own Mac, fast iteration
Push to GitHub, pip install git+https://...	Sharing with team
Publish to PyPI	Open source, public packages
Copy file between projects	Quick and dirty — avoid this
Since it's just for your own projects right now, pip install -e . is the simplest and best approach. Want me to show you how to add more utilities like S3 or DynamoDB helpers?

do I need to install cli each time I start new ec2

May 17
No! You only need to install the AWS CLI on your Mac once — you're already running the CLI commands from your Mac, not from the EC2 instance.

How it works:

Your Mac
  └── AWS CLI (installed once) ──→ talks to AWS API ──→ launches/manages EC2
        └── ~/.aws/credentials (your keys, set once)
The only things you do each time you start a new EC2:

Task	Where	How often
AWS CLI	Your Mac	Once ever
AWS credentials	Your Mac	Once ever
setup.sh	Runs on EC2 automatically	Every new instance
SSH into instance	Your Mac terminal	Each session
Get new public IP	AWS CLI on Mac	Each time instance starts
The one thing that changes each time is the public IP:

bash
# Run this on your Mac to get the new IP after starting an instance
aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].[PublicIpAddress,Tags[?Key==`Name`].Value|[0]]' \
  --output table \
  --region us-east-1
Pro tip — set up an Elastic IP to get a permanent IP that never changes even when you stop/start the instance:

bash
# Allocate an Elastic IP
aws ec2 allocate-address --domain vpc --region us-east-1

# Associate it with your instance
aws ec2 associate-address \
  --instance-id i-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --allocation-id eipalloc-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --region us-east-1
Want me to add Elastic IP setup to your launch script?

I am trying to run this command for pip install

That's an S3 command, not a pip command! You need two separate steps:

Step 1 — Download the file from S3:

bash
aws s3 cp s3://mva-python-code/requirements-core.txt .
Step 2 — Then install from it:

bash
pip3 install -r requirements-core.txt
Or combine into one line:

bash
aws s3 cp s3://mva-python-code/requirements-core.txt . && pip3 install -r requirements-core.txt
If you're getting an error on the S3 command, common causes:

Error	Fix
NoCredentialsError	Run aws configure first
AccessDenied	EC2 needs S3 read permission in IAM role
NoSuchBucket	Check bucket name spelling
NoSuchKey	Check file name spelling
Paste the error message if you're getting one and I'll help fix it!

conda py313 env ──has──→ Python 3.13 ──has──→ dateutil
The system CLI can't see packages installed inside your conda environment. Installing awscli inside conda fixes this permanently. Try it and let me know!

fatal error: Unable to locate credentials

Do i need to include creditials each time i start new ec2

May 17
No, you don't need to add credentials each time! The best way is to attach an IAM Role to your EC2 instance — then credentials are automatic, no configuration needed.

Step 1 — Create an IAM role on your Mac:

bash
# Create the role
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Role \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {"Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com"},
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
    }]
  }'

# Attach S3 access
aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Role \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess

# Attach Secrets Manager access
aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Role \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/SecretsManagerReadWrite

# Create instance profile
aws iam create-instance-profile \
  --instance-profile-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile

# Add role to profile
aws iam add-role-to-instance-profile \
  --instance-profile-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile \
  --role-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Role
Step 2 — Attach role to your running instance:

bash
# Get your instance ID
INSTANCE_ID=$(aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].InstanceId' \
  --output text \
  --region us-east-1)

echo $INSTANCE_ID

# Attach the role
aws ec2 associate-iam-instance-profile \
  --instance-id $INSTANCE_ID \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile \
  --region us-east-1
Step 3 — Test on EC2 (no credentials needed!):

bash
aws s3 cp s3://mva-python-code/requirements-core.txt .
Step 4 — Add role to your launch command so future instances get it automatically:

bash
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t2.micro \
  --key-name your-key-pair \
  --security-group-ids sg-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile \
  --region us-east-1
How it works:

EC2 Instance
  └── IAM Role attached ──→ auto credentials ──→ S3, Secrets Manager
        no keys needed!
This is the AWS recommended approach — never store credentials on EC2, always use IAM roles. Try Step 1 and 2 and let me know if you hit any errors!

(base) madhavathavale@Mac aws-setup % aws iam add-role-to-instance-profile \
  --instance-profile-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile \
  --role-name EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Role

If it already has a role — just attach the profile to your EC2 instance:

  --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].InstanceId' \
  --output text \

# Attach profile to instance

Trouble installing numpy as a part of requirements.txt

../meson.build:1:0: ERROR: Unknown compiler(s): [['cc'], ['gcc'], ['clang'], ['nvc'], ['pgcc'], ['icc'], ['icx']]

pip install numpy works

May 17
The issue is that pip install numpy installs a pre-built binary but installing via requirements.txt is trying to build from source and can't find a C compiler.

Fix — force binary install in requirements.txt:

bash
pip install --only-binary=:all: -r requirements.txt
Or fix the requirements.txt itself by pinning to a version that has pre-built binaries:

numpy>=1.24.0
pandas>=2.0.0
matplotlib>=3.7.0
Then install with:

bash
pip install --prefer-binary -r requirements.txt
If you still want to build from source — install the compiler first:

bash
sudo yum install -y gcc gcc-c++ make
pip install -r requirements.txt
Quickest fix right now:

bash
pip install --only-binary=:all: -r requirements.txt
This forces pip to use pre-built wheels for everything — same as what pip install numpy does when it works. Try that and let me know!

I am having a lot fo trouble with versionsgiven in requirements.txt

May 17
Simplest fix — remove all version numbers from requirements.txt and let pip pick the latest compatible versions:

text
numpy
pandas
matplotlib
seaborn
scikit-learn
boto3
anthropic
requests
python-dotenv
jupyter
ipykernel
Then install with:

bash
pip install --prefer-binary -r requirements.txt
--prefer-binary is the key flag — it always picks pre-built wheels over building from source, which avoids all the compiler errors you've been seeing.

Add this to your setup.sh permanently:

bash
pip install --prefer-binary -r requirements.txt
Instead of just:

bash
pip install -r requirements.txt
This approach works because:

No version conflicts to resolve
Always picks pre-built binaries
Gets latest stable versions
No compiler needed
Try it and let me know what happens!

I am having alot of problems with versions mentioned in requirements.txt > I plan to remove version mumbers from the file. Is this a good ided

May 17
It's a good idea for now while you're learning and experimenting, but here's the honest tradeoff:

Pros of removing versions:

No conflicts
Always gets latest stable versions
Much easier to get started
No compiler errors from incompatible builds
Cons of removing versions:

Code may break in future when packages update
Hard to reproduce exact environment on another machine
Two developers may get different versions
Best practice — two separate files:

text
# requirements.txt (no versions - for development/getting started)
numpy
pandas
matplotlib
seaborn
scikit-learn
boto3
anthropic
requests
python-dotenv
jupyter
text
# requirements-lock.txt (exact versions - generated after everything works)
numpy==1.26.4
pandas==2.2.1
...
Generate the lock file after everything is working:

bash
pip freeze > requirements-lock.txt
My recommendation for you right now:

Since you're in learning/experimentation phase → remove versions, get everything working, then run pip freeze to capture exact versions once stable.

bash
pip freeze > requirements-lock.txt
This gives you the best of both worlds — easy setup now, reproducible later.

how do I fix this?

Collecting huggingface-hub (from -r requirements.txt (line 39))
  Downloading huggingface_hub-1.15.0-py3-none-any.whl.metadata (14 kB)
ERROR: Could not find a version that satisfies the requirement idna3.6 (from versions: none)
ERROR: No matching distribution found for idna3.6

May 17
The problem is a missing space between idna and the version number in your requirements.txt.

Find and fix the line:

bash
# See what's in your requirements.txt around that line
grep idna requirements.txt
It probably looks like:

idna3.6        ← wrong, no space
Fix it to:

idna           ← no version (recommended)
# or
idna>=3.6      ← with space and operator
Quick fix — edit the file directly:

bash
sed -i 's/idna3.6/idna/' requirements.txt
Then verify:

bash
grep idna requirements.txt
Then reinstall:

bash
pip install --prefer-binary -r requirements.txt
To find all similar problems in your requirements.txt — version numbers should always have ==, >=, <=, or no version at all:

bash
# Find any lines with version numbers but missing operator
grep -E '^[a-zA-Z].*[0-9]$' requirements.txt
This catches lines like idna3.6, numpy1.24, etc. that are missing the == or >= operator.

# ─── Install Miniconda as ec2-user ───────────────────────────
echo "Installing Miniconda..."
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
cd /home/ec2-user

# Download Miniconda
curl -O https://repo.anaconda.com/miniconda/Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh

# Install silently
bash Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh -b -p /home/ec2-user/miniconda3

# Remove installer
rm Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh

# Add conda to PATH
echo 'export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
echo 'source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh

# Accept conda ToS
conda tos accept --override-channels --channel https://repo.anaconda.com/pkgs/main
conda tos accept --override-channels --channel https://repo.anaconda.com/pkgs/r

# Add conda-forge channel
conda config --add channels conda-forge
conda config --set channel_priority strict

echo "Miniconda installed"
EOF

# ─── Create Python 3.13 environment ──────────────────────────
echo "Creating Python 3.13 environment..."
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh

# Create environment
conda create -n py313 python=3.13 -y

echo "Python 3.13 environment created"
EOF

# ─── Download requirements.txt from S3 ───────────────────────
echo "Downloading requirements.txt from S3..."
aws s3 cp s3://mva-python-code/requirements-core.txt /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt \
  --region us-east-1

# Strip version numbers and fix common issues
sed -i 's/==[0-9].*//' /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt   # remove ==x.x.x
sed -i 's/>=[0-9].*//' /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt   # remove >=x.x.x
sed -i 's/<=[0-9].*//' /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt   # remove <=x.x.x
sed -i 's/\([a-zA-Z]\)[0-9].*/\1/' /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt  # fix idna3.6 style

echo "requirements.txt downloaded and cleaned"
cat /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt

# ─── Install Python packages ──────────────────────────────────
echo "Installing Python packages..."
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh
conda activate py313

# Install from requirements.txt
pip install --prefer-binary -r /home/ec2-user/requirements-core.txt

# Install essential packages not in requirements.txt
pip install --prefer-binary \
  boto3 \
  anthropic \
  python-dotenv \
  ipykernel

echo "Python packages installed"
EOF

# ─── Fetch API keys from Secrets Manager ─────────────────────
echo "Fetching API keys..."
AWS_REGION="us-east-1"

WEATHER_KEY=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id "Weather_API_KEY" \
  --region $AWS_REGION \
  --query 'SecretString' \
  --output text 2>/dev/null)

if [ ! -z "$WEATHER_KEY" ]; then
  # Extract key from JSON
  WEATHER_KEY_VALUE=$(echo $WEATHER_KEY | python3 -c "import sys,json; print(json.loads(sys.stdin.read())['Weather_API_KEY'])")
  echo "export WEATHER_API_KEY=$WEATHER_KEY_VALUE" >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc
  echo "Weather API key fetched"
else
  echo "WARNING: Could not fetch Weather API key"
fi

# ─── Create project folder structure ─────────────────────────
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/projects
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/data
mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/scripts
echo "Folder structure created"
EOF

# ─── Auto activate py313 on login ────────────────────────────
echo 'conda activate py313' >> /home/ec2-user/.bashrc

# ─── Verify installations ─────────────────────────────────────
echo "─── Verifying installations ───"
sudo -u ec2-user bash << 'EOF'
export PATH=/home/ec2-user/miniconda3/bin:$PATH
source /home/ec2-user/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh
conda activate py313

echo "Python version:"
python --version

echo "Installed packages:"
python -c "
import importlib
packages = ['boto3', 'anthropic', 'requests', 'dotenv']
for pkg in packages:
    try:
        importlib.import_module(pkg)
        print(f'  ✓ {pkg}')
    except ImportError:
        print(f'  ✗ {pkg} MISSING')
"
EOF

echo "─── Setup complete at $(date) ───"
Save it and make executable:

bash
chmod +x setup.sh
Launch your EC2 instance with the IAM role attached:

bash
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t3.small \
  --key-name your-key-pair \
  --security-group-ids sg-xxxxxxxxxx \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=EC2-S3-SecretsManager-Profile \
  --user-data file://setup.sh \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=instance,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=my-server}]'
After launching — check the log:

bash
sudo cat /var/log/setup.log

Fix	Reason
AWS CLI v2	No Python dependency issues
Miniconda + Python 3.13	No building from source
conda-forge channel	No ToS issues
--prefer-binary	No compiler errors
Strip version numbers	No conflict errors
IAM role in launch command	No credentials needed
Full logging	Easy to debug
