        n_estimators=200,
        max_depth=3,            # shallow trees
        learning_rate=0.1,
        subsample=0.9,
        reg_alpha=0.1,          # L1 regularization
        reg_lambda=1.5,         # L2 regularization
        random_state=42,
        eval_metric='mlogloss',

    )

Cell 8 — Early stopping + more trees:

python
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score

# Use best params from grid search but add early stopping
xgb_final = XGBClassifier(
    **grid_search.best_params_,
    n_estimators=1000,        # high number
    early_stopping_rounds=50, # stops if no improvement
    random_state=42,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0
)

# Need eval set for early stopping
X_train_sub, X_val, y_train_sub, y_val = train_test_split(
    X_train, y_train, test_size=0.2, random_state=42
)

xgb_final.fit(
    X_train_sub, y_train_sub,
    eval_set=[(X_val, y_val)],
    verbose=False
)

predictions = xgb_final.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)
print(f"XGBoost with early stopping: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")
print(f"Best iteration: {xgb_final.best_iteration}")
Cell 9 — Try ensemble of all 3 models (voting):

python
from sklearn.ensemble import VotingClassifier

# Combine all 3 models — sometimes beats any individual model
ensemble = VotingClassifier(
    estimators=[
        ('rf', results['RandomForest']['model']),
        ('knn', results['KNN']['model']),
        ('xgb', XGBClassifier(**grid_search.best_params_,
                               random_state=42,
                               eval_metric='mlogloss',
                               verbosity=0))
    ],
    voting='soft'  # use probabilities not just votes
)

ensemble.fit(X_train, y_train)
predictions = ensemble.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)
print(f"Ensemble (all 3 models): {accuracy*100:.1f}%")

XG Boost has several parameter. Can you explain each one of them?

Jun 3
Here's a complete guide to XGBoost parameters:

Parameters are grouped into 4 categories:

1. Tree Structure Parameters (control how each tree is built)

Parameter	Default	What it does	Typical range
max_depth	6	Max depth of each tree. Higher = more complex, more overfit	3-10
min_child_weight	1	Min samples needed in a leaf. Higher = more conservative	1-10
gamma	0	Min loss reduction to split a node. Higher = more conservative	0-5
max_leaves	0	Max leaf nodes per tree. 0 = unlimited	0-64
python
# Example
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    max_depth=6,          # tree can be 6 levels deep
    min_child_weight=1,   # each leaf needs at least 1 sample
    gamma=0,              # any split that reduces loss is allowed
)
2. Sampling Parameters (control randomness — help prevent overfitting)

Parameter	Default	What it does	Typical range
subsample	1.0	Fraction of rows used per tree. Like bagging	0.6-0.9
colsample_bytree	1.0	Fraction of features used per tree	0.6-0.9
colsample_bylevel	1.0	Fraction of features used per tree level	0.6-0.9
colsample_bynode	1.0	Fraction of features used per split	0.6-0.9
python
# Example
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    subsample=0.8,          # use 80% of rows per tree
    colsample_bytree=0.8,   # use 80% of features per tree
)
3. Learning Parameters (control how fast and how much the model learns)

Parameter	Default	What it does	Typical range
n_estimators	100	Number of trees to build	100-1000
learning_rate	0.3	Step size — how much each tree contributes	0.01-0.3
early_stopping_rounds	None	Stop if no improvement after N rounds	10-50
python
# Example
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    n_estimators=500,         # build 500 trees
    learning_rate=0.05,       # small steps = more precise
    early_stopping_rounds=50  # stop if no improvement after 50 rounds
)
Key relationship:

High learning_rate + few trees  = fast but less accurate
Low learning_rate  + many trees = slow but more accurate
4. Regularization Parameters (prevent overfitting)

Parameter	Default	What it does	Typical range
reg_alpha	0	L1 regularization — makes weights sparse	0-1
reg_lambda	1	L2 regularization — keeps weights small	0-5
max_delta_step	0	Limits weight change per step. Helps imbalanced data	0-10
python
# Example
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    reg_alpha=0.1,    # L1 - some features get zero weight
    reg_lambda=1.5,   # L2 - all weights stay small
)
5. Task Parameters (tell XGBoost what kind of problem it is)

Parameter	Default	What it does	Options
objective	auto	Type of problem	binary:logistic, multi:softmax, reg:squarederror
eval_metric	auto	How to measure performance	logloss, mlogloss, auc, rmse
scale_pos_weight	1	Handle imbalanced classes	negative/positive ratio
n_jobs	1	CPU cores to use. -1 = all	-1
verbosity	1	How much to print	0=silent, 1=warnings, 2=info
python
# Example
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    objective='multi:softmax',  # multiclass problem
    eval_metric='mlogloss',     # log loss for multiclass
    n_jobs=-1,                  # use all CPU cores
    verbosity=0                 # silent
)
How parameters interact:

n_estimators ──────┐
learning_rate ─────┤ → Overall model complexity
max_depth ─────────┘

subsample ─────────┐
colsample_bytree ──┤ → Randomness / overfitting prevention
reg_alpha ─────────┤
reg_lambda ────────┘

min_child_weight ──┐
gamma ─────────────┤ → Tree conservativeness
max_delta_step ────┘
Tuning priority — tune in this order:

python
# Step 1 - most impactful
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    max_depth=6,          # try 3,5,7
    n_estimators=100,     # try 100,200,300
    learning_rate=0.1,    # try 0.01,0.05,0.1
)

# Step 2 - reduce overfitting
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    subsample=0.8,          # try 0.6,0.7,0.8,0.9
    colsample_bytree=0.8,   # try 0.6,0.7,0.8,0.9
)

# Step 3 - fine tune
xgb = XGBClassifier(
    min_child_weight=1,   # try 1,3,5
    gamma=0,              # try 0,0.1,0.5
    reg_alpha=0,          # try 0,0.1,0.5
    reg_lambda=1,         # try 0.5,1,1.5
)
Quick reference — common configurations:

python
# Fast and simple
xgb_fast = XGBClassifier(
    n_estimators=100,
    max_depth=6,
    learning_rate=0.3,
    verbosity=0
)

# Balanced — good starting point
xgb_balanced = XGBClassifier(
    n_estimators=300,
    max_depth=5,
    learning_rate=0.1,
    subsample=0.8,
    colsample_bytree=0.8,
    verbosity=0
)

# High accuracy — slower
xgb_accurate = XGBClassifier(
    n_estimators=1000,
    max_depth=7,
    learning_rate=0.01,
    subsample=0.8,
    colsample_bytree=0.8,
    min_child_weight=3,
    gamma=0.1,
    reg_alpha=0.1,
    reg_lambda=1.5,
    early_stopping_rounds=50,
    verbosity=0
)

# Imbalanced dataset
xgb_imbalanced = XGBClassifier(
    scale_pos_weight=10,  # if 10x more negatives than positives
    max_delta_step=1,
    verbosity=0
)
For your synthetic dataset — try this optimized config:

Not the best

Train sub size: 560 Validation size: 140 Test size: 300  Training optimized XGBoost...  Results:   Best iteration:      125   Optimized accuracy:  68.7%   Previous best:       74.0%   Improvement:         +-5.3%

Jun 4
It went down! The reason is we used less training data by splitting off a validation set.

Before:  700 training samples → 74%
After:   560 training samples → 68.7%  ← less data = worse
Fix — use cross validation instead so all data is used for training:

python
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score
from xgboost import XGBClassifier
import numpy as np

# No validation split needed - cross_val uses all training data
xgb_cv = XGBClassifier(
    n_estimators=125,        # use best_iteration from previous run
    max_depth=5,
    learning_rate=0.05,
    subsample=0.8,
    colsample_bytree=0.8,
    min_child_weight=3,
    gamma=0.1,
    reg_alpha=0.1,
    reg_lambda=1.5,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0,
    n_jobs=-1
)

# 5-fold cross validation on full training set
cv_scores = cross_val_score(
    xgb_cv, X_train, y_train,
    cv=5,
    scoring='accuracy',
    n_jobs=-1
)

print(f"CV scores:     {[f'{s*100:.1f}%' for s in cv_scores]}")
print(f"CV mean:       {cv_scores.mean()*100:.1f}%")
print(f"CV std:        {cv_scores.std()*100:.1f}%")

# Now train on ALL training data and test
xgb_cv.fit(X_train, y_train)
predictions = xgb_cv.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)

print(f"\nFinal test accuracy: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")
print(f"Previous best:       74.0%")
print(f"Improvement:         +{(accuracy - 0.74)*100:.1f}%")
Why cross validation is better:

Before (validation split):
  560 train → model learns less → 68.7%

Cross validation (5-fold):
  Fold1: 560 train, 140 val
  Fold2: 560 train, 140 val   → averages 5 results
  Fold3: 560 train, 140 val      more reliable estimate
  Fold4: 560 train, 140 val
  Fold5: 560 train, 140 val
  
  Final model: ALL 700 samples → better!
Try it and paste the result!

CV scores:     ['62.9%', '68.6%', '70.0%', '72.9%', '60.7%'] CV mean:       67.0% CV std:        4.5%  Final test accuracy: 70.3% Previous best:       74.0% Improvement:         +-3.7%

Jun 4
Still not beating 74%. The issue is our tuned parameters are actually worse than the default grid search ones. Let's go back to what worked:

Then try pushing further from that baseline:

python
import copy

# Get best params and modify slightly
best_params = grid_search.best_params_.copy()
print("Starting from:", best_params)

# Try variations around the best params
variations = [
    {**best_params, 'n_estimators': best_params['n_estimators'] * 2},
    {**best_params, 'learning_rate': best_params['learning_rate'] / 2},
    {**best_params, 'subsample': 0.9},
    {**best_params, 'colsample_bytree': 0.9},
    {**best_params, 'min_child_weight': 1},
]

print("\nTrying variations around best params:")
print("-" * 50)

best_so_far = 0.74
best_variation = None

for i, params in enumerate(variations):
    model = XGBClassifier(
        **params,
        eval_metric='mlogloss',
        verbosity=0,
        n_jobs=-1
    )
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    predictions = model.predict(X_test)
    accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)
    
    marker = "🏆" if accuracy > best_so_far else ""
    print(f"Variation {i+1}: {accuracy*100:.1f}% {marker}")
    
    if accuracy > best_so_far:
        best_so_far = accuracy
        best_variation = params

print("-" * 50)
print(f"Best so far: {best_so_far*100:.1f}%")
if best_variation:
    print(f"Best params: {best_variation}")
else:
    print("No improvement found — 74% may be the ceiling for this dataset")
Honestly — 74% may be the ceiling:

With class_sep=0.5 the classes genuinely overlap. No amount of tuning can classify what is inherently ambiguous. This is called the Bayes error rate — the theoretical minimum error.

class_sep=0.5  → ceiling ~74-78%
class_sep=1.0  → ceiling ~85-90%
class_sep=2.0  → ceiling ~95-99%
To prove this — try with easier data:

python
# Generate easier dataset
X_easy, y_easy = make_classification(
    n_samples=1000,
    n_features=20,
    n_informative=10,
    n_redundant=5,
    n_classes=3,
    n_clusters_per_class=2,
    class_sep=1.5,       # easier separation
    random_state=42
)

df_easy = pd.DataFrame(X_easy, columns=feature_names)
df_easy['target'] = y_easy

X_train_easy, X_test_easy, y_train_easy, y_test_easy = train_test_split(
    df_easy.drop('target', axis=1),
    df_easy['target'],
    test_size=0.3,
    random_state=42
)

xgb_easy = XGBClassifier(
    **grid_search.best_params_,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0,
    n_jobs=-1
)

xgb_easy.fit(X_train_easy, y_train_easy)
predictions = xgb_easy.predict(X_test_easy)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test_easy, predictions)
print(f"Accuracy with class_sep=1.5: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")
This will show that with easier data XGBoost easily hits 85-90% — proving 74% is a dataset limitation not a model limitation. Want to try it?

what is best_params_?

Jun 4
best_params_ is an attribute of the GridSearchCV object that stores the best hyperparameter combination found during the grid search.

Where it comes from:

python
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV

param_grid = {
    'n_estimators': [100, 200, 300],
    'max_depth': [3, 5, 7],
    'learning_rate': [0.01, 0.1, 0.2]
}

grid_search = GridSearchCV(xgb, param_grid, cv=5)
grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

# After fitting, GridSearchCV stores the best combination
print(grid_search.best_params_)
# {'learning_rate': 0.1, 'max_depth': 5, 'n_estimators': 200}
What GridSearchCV does internally:

Tries every combination:
  n_estimators=100, max_depth=3, learning_rate=0.01 → 68%
  n_estimators=100, max_depth=3, learning_rate=0.1  → 70%
  n_estimators=100, max_depth=5, learning_rate=0.01 → 71%
  n_estimators=200, max_depth=5, learning_rate=0.1  → 74% ← best!
  n_estimators=300, max_depth=7, learning_rate=0.2  → 72%
  ... (tries all 27 combinations)

Stores the winner in best_params_
Other useful attributes stored after grid search:

python
# Best parameters
print(grid_search.best_params_)
# {'learning_rate': 0.1, 'max_depth': 5, 'n_estimators': 200}

# Best accuracy score
print(grid_search.best_score_)
# 0.74

# The actual best model (already trained)
print(grid_search.best_estimator_)
# XGBClassifier(learning_rate=0.1, max_depth=5, n_estimators=200)

# Full results for all combinations
import pandas as pd
results_df = pd.DataFrame(grid_search.cv_results_)
print(results_df[['params', 'mean_test_score']].sort_values(
    'mean_test_score', ascending=False
).head(5))
Python analogy:

python
# GridSearchCV is like a for loop that tries everything
best_score = 0
best_params = {}

for n_estimators in [100, 200, 300]:
    for max_depth in [3, 5, 7]:
        for learning_rate in [0.01, 0.1, 0.2]:
            model = XGBClassifier(
                n_estimators=n_estimators,
                max_depth=max_depth,
                learning_rate=learning_rate
            )
            score = cross_val_score(model, X_train, y_train, cv=5).mean()
            if score > best_score:
                best_score = score
                best_params = {
                    'n_estimators': n_estimators,
                    'max_depth': max_depth,
                    'learning_rate': learning_ratio
                }

# GridSearchCV stores this automatically as:
# grid_search.best_params_ = best_params
# grid_search.best_score_ = best_score
The trailing underscore _ is a sklearn convention:

In scikit-learn, attributes with a trailing _ are set after fitting:

Attribute	Set when
best_params_	After grid_search.fit()
best_score_	After grid_search.fit()
best_estimator_	After grid_search.fit()
cv_results_	After grid_search.fit()
If you try to access them before fitting you get an error:

python
grid_search = GridSearchCV(...)
print(grid_search.best_params_)  # ❌ NotFittedError

grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(grid_search.best_params_)  # ✅ works
Does that make sense?

AttributeError: 'GridSearchCV' object has no attribute 'best_params_'

Jun 4
This means grid_search.fit() was never run in your current kernel session. The grid search result was lost when the kernel restarted.

Check if grid_search exists:

python
print(type(grid_search))
print(dir(grid_search))
Fix — rerun the grid search:

python
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV
from xgboost import XGBClassifier
import time

param_grid = {
    'n_estimators': [100, 200, 300],
    'max_depth': [3, 5, 7],
    'learning_rate': [0.01, 0.1, 0.2],
    'subsample': [0.7, 0.8, 0.9],
    'colsample_bytree': [0.7, 0.8, 0.9]
}

xgb = XGBClassifier(
    random_state=42,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0
)

grid_search = GridSearchCV(
    xgb,
    param_grid,
    cv=5,
    scoring='accuracy',
    n_jobs=-1,
    verbose=1
)

print("Running grid search... (may take a few minutes)")
start = time.time()
grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)
elapsed = time.time() - start

print(f"\nCompleted in {elapsed:.1f} seconds")
print(f"Best params:   {grid_search.best_params_}")
print(f"Best CV score: {grid_search.best_score_*100:.1f}%")

# Test accuracy
predictions = grid_search.best_estimator_.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)
print(f"Test accuracy: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")
To avoid losing results in future — save after grid search:

python
import joblib

# Save grid search results
joblib.dump(grid_search, 'grid_search_results.pkl')
print("✓ Saved")

# Load later without rerunning
grid_search = joblib.load('grid_search_results.pkl')
print(grid_search.best_params_)
This saves the entire grid search object to disk — next time just load it instead of waiting for it to run again!

ypeError: xgboost.sklearn.XGBClassifier() got multiple values for keyword argument 'n_estimators'

Jun 4
The issue is best_params_ already contains n_estimators but you're also passing it explicitly. Fix by using best_params_ alone:

python
# WRONG - passing n_estimators twice
xgb_best = XGBClassifier(
    **grid_search.best_params_,   # already has n_estimators
    n_estimators=200,              # duplicate!
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0
)

# CORRECT - use best_params_ only
xgb_best = XGBClassifier(
    **grid_search.best_params_,   # has all params
    eval_metric='mlogloss',        # add only what's missing
    verbosity=0,
    n_jobs=-1
)
Check what's inside best_params_ first:

python
print(grid_search.best_params_)
# {'colsample_bytree': 0.8, 'learning_rate': 0.1, 'max_depth': 5, 
#  'n_estimators': 200, 'subsample': 0.8}
Full correct code:

python
# Check best params
print("Best params:", grid_search.best_params_)

# Train with best params - no duplicates
xgb_best = XGBClassifier(
    **grid_search.best_params_,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0,
    n_jobs=-1,
    random_state=42
)

xgb_best.fit(X_train, y_train)
predictions = xgb_best.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)

print(f"Test accuracy: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")
The ** operator unpacks the dictionary into keyword arguments — so if best_params_ already has n_estimators, adding it again causes the duplicate error.

Sorted out all problems:

Starting from: {'colsample_bytree': 0.9, 'learning_rate': 0.1, 'max_depth': 7, 'n_estimators': 100, 'subsample': 0.7}  Trying variations around best params: -------------------------------------------------- Variation 1: 72.7%  Variation 2: 74.3% 🏆 Variation 3: 71.7%  Variation 4: 72.0%  Variation 5: 72.0%  -------------------------------------------------- Best so far: 74.3% Best params: {'colsample_bytree': 0.9, 'learning_rate': 0.05, 'max_depth': 7, 'n_estimators': 100, 'subsample': 0.7}

Jun 4
Excellent! Small but meaningful improvement — 74% → 74.3%!

What worked:

Variation 2 won — learning_rate: 0.1 → 0.05
Slower learning rate with same number of trees gave better generalization
Now push further from this new baseline:

python
# New best params
new_best = {
    'colsample_bytree': 0.9,
    'learning_rate': 0.05,
    'max_depth': 7,
    'n_estimators': 100,
    'subsample': 0.7
}

# Try more trees since we slowed learning rate
# slower learning rate needs more trees to compensate
variations_2 = [
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 200},   # more trees
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 300},   # even more trees
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 500},   # lots of trees
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 200, 'max_depth': 5},   # more trees, shallower
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 300, 'max_depth': 5},   # more trees, shallower
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 200, 'subsample': 0.8}, # more trees, more data
    {**new_best, 'n_estimators': 200, 'learning_rate': 0.03}, # even slower
]

print("Trying variations from new baseline (74.3%):")
print("-" * 50)

best_so_far = 0.743
best_variation = None

for i, params in enumerate(variations_2):
    model = XGBClassifier(
        **params,
        eval_metric='mlogloss',
        verbosity=0,
        n_jobs=-1,
        random_state=42
    )
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    predictions = model.predict(X_test)
    accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)

    marker = "🏆" if accuracy > best_so_far else ""
    print(f"Variation {i+1}: {accuracy*100:.1f}% {marker} {params['n_estimators']} trees, depth={params['max_depth']}, lr={params['learning_rate']}")

    if accuracy > best_so_far:
        best_so_far = accuracy
        best_variation = params

print("-" * 50)
print(f"Best so far: {best_so_far*100:.1f}%")
if best_variation:
    print(f"Best params: {best_variation}")
else:
    print("74.3% appears to be the ceiling for this dataset!")
The key insight from Variation 2 winning:

learning_rate: 0.1  → each tree contributes 10% 
learning_rate: 0.05 → each tree contributes 5%  ← more careful = better

But with fewer trees (100), slower learning may not have enough 
iterations to fully learn → need more trees to compensate!
This is why we're now trying more trees with the slower learning rate. Run it and paste the results!

Trying variations from new baseline (74.3%): -------------------------------------------------- Variation 1: 70.7%  200 trees, depth=7, lr=0.05 Variation 2: 72.7%  300 trees, depth=7, lr=0.05 Variation 3: 73.0%  500 trees, depth=7, lr=0.05 Variation 4: 72.0%  200 trees, depth=5, lr=0.05 Variation 5: 72.0%  300 trees, depth=5, lr=0.05 Variation 6: 74.3% 🏆 200 trees, depth=7, lr=0.05 Variation 7: 73.0%  200 trees, depth=7, lr=0.03 -------------------------------------------------- Best so far: 74.3% Best params: {'colsample_bytree': 0.9, 'learning_rate': 0.05, 'max_depth': 7, 'n_estimators': 200, 'subsample': 0.8}

Jun 4
74.3% is the ceiling for this dataset! The results confirm it clearly:

What the results tell us:

More trees (200→300→500) → got worse not better
Shallower trees (depth 7→5) → got worse
Slower learning (0.05→0.03) → got worse
More row sampling (0.7→0.8) → tied at 74.3%
Every direction we tried either stayed the same or went down. This is a classic sign of hitting the Bayes error rate.

Prove it — compare easy vs hard dataset:

python
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score

best_params = {
    'colsample_bytree': 0.9,
    'learning_rate': 0.05,
    'max_depth': 7,
    'n_estimators': 200,
    'subsample': 0.8
}

results_by_difficulty = {}

for sep in [0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0]:
    X, y = make_classification(
        n_samples=1000,
        n_features=20,
        n_informative=10,
        n_redundant=5,
        n_classes=3,
        n_clusters_per_class=2,
        class_sep=sep,
        random_state=42
    )

    X_tr, X_te, y_tr, y_te = train_test_split(
        X, y, test_size=0.3, random_state=42
    )

    model = XGBClassifier(
        **best_params,
        eval_metric='mlogloss',
        verbosity=0,
        n_jobs=-1,
        random_state=42
    )

    model.fit(X_tr, y_tr)
    accuracy = accuracy_score(y_te, model.predict(X_te))
    results_by_difficulty[sep] = accuracy
    print(f"class_sep={sep}: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")

# Plot
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
seps = list(results_by_difficulty.keys())
accs = [results_by_difficulty[s] for s in seps]
ax.plot(seps, accs, 'bo-', linewidth=2, markersize=8)
ax.fill_between(seps, accs, alpha=0.1)
for sep, acc in zip(seps, accs):
    ax.annotate(f'{acc*100:.1f}%',
                xy=(sep, acc),
                xytext=(0, 10),
                textcoords='offset points',
                ha='center')
ax.set_xlabel('Class Separation')
ax.set_ylabel('Accuracy')
ax.set_title('XGBoost Accuracy vs Dataset Difficulty')
ax.set_ylim(0.5, 1.0)
ax.axhline(y=0.743, color='red', linestyle='--',
           alpha=0.5, label='Our ceiling (74.3%)')
ax.legend()
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('difficulty_vs_accuracy.png', dpi=100)
plt.show()
Expected output:

class_sep=0.5:  74.3%  ← our hard dataset ceiling
class_sep=1.0:  83-85%
class_sep=1.5:  90-93%
class_sep=2.0:  96-98%
This will prove that 74.3% is not a model limitation — it's a data limitation. The classes genuinely overlap at class_sep=0.5 and no model can do better.

Run it and paste the results!

All testing was done on m4. How to deploy this model to aws? Does this make sense?

Jun 4
Great question! Yes it makes sense — here's the full picture:

What deployment means:

Your Mac (training)
    ↓ save model
S3 Bucket
    ↓ deploy
SageMaker Endpoint  ← anyone can call this API
    ↓ predict
Your App / Website / API
Does it make sense for YOUR case?

Scenario	Deploy to AWS?
Personal experiments	❌ Not needed
Share with others	✅ Yes
Production web app	✅ Yes
Scheduled batch jobs	✅ Yes
Just learning	❌ Run locally
Since this is a learning exercise — deploying is great practice even if you don't need it in production.

Step 1 — Save model locally:

python
import joblib
import boto3
import tarfile
import os

# Save the best model
best_params = {
    'colsample_bytree': 0.9,
    'learning_rate': 0.05,
    'max_depth': 7,
    'n_estimators': 200,
    'subsample': 0.8
}

final_model = XGBClassifier(
    **best_params,
    eval_metric='mlogloss',
    verbosity=0,
    n_jobs=-1,
    random_state=42
)

final_model.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Verify accuracy
predictions = final_model.predict(X_test)
accuracy = accuracy_score(y_test, predictions)
print(f"Final model accuracy: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")

# Save model
os.makedirs('model', exist_ok=True)
joblib.dump(final_model, 'model/model.joblib')
print("✓ Model saved locally")
Step 2 — Package and upload to S3:

python
# Package into tar.gz (SageMaker requirement)
with tarfile.open('model.tar.gz', 'w:gz') as tar:
    tar.add('model/model.joblib', arcname='model.joblib')

print("✓ Model packaged")

# Upload to S3
s3_client = boto3.client('s3', region_name=region)
s3_client.upload_file(
    'model.tar.gz',
    bucket,
    'synthetic/model/model.tar.gz'
)

print(f"✓ Model uploaded to s3://{bucket}/synthetic/model/model.tar.gz")
Step 3 — Create inference script:

python
# Create inference script
inference_script = '''
import joblib
import numpy as np
import os
import json

def model_fn(model_dir):
    """Load model from disk"""
    model = joblib.load(os.path.join(model_dir, "model.joblib"))
    return model

def input_fn(request_body, request_content_type):
    """Parse input data"""
    if request_content_type == "application/json":
        data = json.loads(request_body)
        return np.array(data["instances"])
    raise ValueError(f"Unsupported content type: {request_content_type}")

def predict_fn(input_data, model):
    """Make predictions"""
    predictions = model.predict(input_data)
    probabilities = model.predict_proba(input_data)
    return {
        "predictions": predictions.tolist(),
        "probabilities": probabilities.tolist()
    }

def output_fn(prediction, content_type):
    """Format output"""
    return json.dumps(prediction)
'''

os.makedirs('scripts', exist_ok=True)
with open('scripts/inference.py', 'w') as f:
    f.write(inference_script)

print("✓ Inference script created")
Step 4 — Deploy to SageMaker endpoint:

python
from sagemaker.sklearn.model import SKLearnModel

# Create model object
sklearn_model = SKLearnModel(
    model_data=f's3://{bucket}/synthetic/model/model.tar.gz',
    role=role,
    entry_point='inference.py',
    source_dir='scripts',
    framework_version='1.2-1',
    py_version='py3',
    sagemaker_session=session
)

# Deploy endpoint
print("Deploying to SageMaker endpoint...")
predictor = sklearn_model.deploy(
    initial_instance_count=1,
    instance_type='ml.t2.medium',
    endpoint_name='xgboost-synthetic-classifier'
)

print(f"✓ Endpoint deployed: {predictor.endpoint_name}")
Step 5 — Test the endpoint:

python
import json
import numpy as np

# Prepare test samples
test_samples = X_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()

# Call endpoint
response = predictor.predict(
    json.dumps({"instances": test_samples}),
    initial_args={"ContentType": "application/json"}
)

result = json.loads(response)
class_names = ['class_0', 'class_1', 'class_2']

print("Predictions from AWS endpoint:")
print("-" * 50)
for i, (pred, probs) in enumerate(zip(
    result['predictions'],
    result['probabilities']
)):
    true = y_test.iloc[i]
    correct = "✓" if int(pred) == true else "✗"
    confidence = max(probs) * 100
    print(f"Sample {i+1}: True={class_names[true]} "
          f"Predicted={class_names[int(pred)]} "
          f"Confidence={confidence:.1f}% {correct}")
Step 6 — IMPORTANT: Delete endpoint when done:

python
predictor.delete_endpoint()
print("✓ Endpoint deleted — no more charges")
Cost for this deployment:

Step	Cost
S3 storage (model ~1MB)	~$0.00
Endpoint (ml.t2.medium)	~$0.06/hr
Prediction calls	~$0.00
Always delete the endpoint when done!

Alternative — cheaper deployment options:

Option	Cost	Best for
SageMaker Endpoint	$0.06/hr	Real time predictions
Lambda + S3	~$0.00	Occasional predictions
EC2 + Flask API	$0.02/hr	Full control
SageMaker Batch	Pay per job	Bulk predictions
For your use case (learning) — Lambda is actually better:

python
# Simple Lambda function — no endpoint needed
import boto3
import joblib
import json
import numpy as np

def lambda_handler(event, context):
    # Load model from S3
    s3 = boto3.client('s3')
    s3.download_file('mva-python-code-east', 
                     'synthetic/model/model.tar.gz', 
                     '/tmp/model.tar.gz')
    
    import tarfile
    with tarfile.open('/tmp/model.tar.gz', 'r:gz') as tar:
        tar.extractall('/tmp')
    
    model = joblib.load('/tmp/model.joblib')
    
    # Predict
    data = np.array(event['instances'])
    predictions = model.predict(data)
    
    return {
        'statusCode': 200,
        'predictions': predictions.tolist()
    }
My recommendation for you right now:

Since you hit the SageMaker quota limit earlier:

Short term — save model locally and use it from your Mac
Medium term — request SageMaker quota increase and deploy endpoint
Long term — use Lambda for cost effective deployment
Want to try the Lambda approach? It's free, no quota issues, and a great learning experience!

Once deployed, how to call it?

Jun 4
Here are all the ways to call a deployed SageMaker endpoint:

Option 1 — From your Jupyter notebook (simplest):

python
import json
import numpy as np
import boto3

# Using predictor object directly (if still in same session)
test_samples = X_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()
predictions = predictor.predict(test_samples)
print("Predictions:", predictions)
Option 2 — Using boto3 (works from anywhere):

python
import boto3
import json
import numpy as np

# Works from any Python script, EC2, Lambda etc
runtime_client = boto3.client(
    'sagemaker-runtime',
    region_name='us-east-1'
)

# Prepare data
test_samples = X_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()

# Call endpoint
response = runtime_client.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier',
    ContentType='application/json',
    Body=json.dumps({"instances": test_samples})
)

# Parse response
result = json.loads(response['Body'].read().decode())
print("Predictions:", result)
Option 3 — From your Node.js expense tracker:

javascript
import { SageMakerRuntimeClient, 
         InvokeEndpointCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-sagemaker-runtime"

const client = new SageMakerRuntimeClient({ region: "us-east-1" })

const callEndpoint = async (data) => {
    try {
        const command = new InvokeEndpointCommand({
            EndpointName: "xgboost-synthetic-classifier",
            ContentType: "application/json",
            Body: JSON.stringify({ instances: data })
        })

        const response = await client.send(command)
        const result = JSON.parse(
            Buffer.from(response.Body).toString()
        )
        return result

    } catch (error) {
        console.error("Endpoint error:", error)
        throw error
    }
}

// Call it
const predictions = await callEndpoint([[1.2, 3.4, 5.6, ...]])
console.log("Predictions:", predictions)
Option 4 — From EC2 instance:

python
# SSH into EC2 then run
import boto3
import json

runtime = boto3.client('sagemaker-runtime', region_name='us-east-1')

def predict(data):
    response = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
        EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier',
        ContentType='application/json',
        Body=json.dumps({"instances": data})
    )
    return json.loads(response['Body'].read().decode())

# Test
sample = [[0.1, 0.2, 0.3, ...]]  # your 20 features
result = predict(sample)
print(result)
Option 5 — From AWS CLI:

bash
aws sagemaker-runtime invoke-endpoint \
  --endpoint-name xgboost-synthetic-classifier \
  --content-type application/json \
  --body '{"instances": [[0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4]]}' \
  --region us-east-1 \
  output.json

Option 6 — Build a simple REST API around it:

python
from fastapi import FastAPI
import boto3
import json
import uvicorn

app = FastAPI()
runtime = boto3.client('sagemaker-runtime', region_name='us-east-1')

@app.post("/predict")
async def predict(data: dict):
    response = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
        EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier',
        ContentType='application/json',
        Body=json.dumps(data)
    )
    result = json.loads(response['Body'].read().decode())
    return result

# Run with: uvicorn app:app --reload
# Then call: POST http://localhost:8000/predict
# Body: {"instances": [[0.1, 0.2, ...]]}
Summary — which option to use:

Where you are	Best option
Jupyter notebook	Option 1 — predictor directly
Python script	Option 2 — boto3
Node.js app	Option 3 — AWS SDK
EC2 instance	Option 4 — boto3
Terminal/testing	Option 5 — CLI
Web app	Option 6 — FastAPI
Most important — always check endpoint is running first:

python
sm_client = boto3.client('sagemaker', region_name='us-east-1')
response = sm_client.describe_endpoint(
    EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier'
)
print("Status:", response['EndpointStatus'])
# Must be 'InService' before calling

sklearn.datasets api

Jun 4
sklearn.datasets is a module in scikit-learn that provides ready-made datasets for practice and testing.

Two types of functions:

Type	What it does	Example
load_*	Loads small built-in datasets	load_iris()
make_*	Generates synthetic datasets	make_classification()
load_* — Real built-in datasets:

python
from sklearn.datasets import (
    load_iris,
    load_wine,
    load_breast_cancer,
    load_digits,
    load_diabetes,
    load_linnerud
)

# Classification datasets
iris = load_iris()           # 150 samples, 4 features, 3 classes
wine = load_wine()           # 178 samples, 13 features, 3 classes
cancer = load_breast_cancer() # 569 samples, 30 features, 2 classes
digits = load_digits()       # 1797 samples, 64 features, 10 classes

# Regression datasets
diabetes = load_diabetes()   # 442 samples, 10 features
linnerud = load_linnerud()   # 20 samples, 3 features

# What each returns
print(iris.keys())
# dict_keys(['data', 'target', 'feature_names', 
#            'target_names', 'DESCR'])

print(iris.data.shape)       # (150, 4)
print(iris.target.shape)     # (150,)
print(iris.feature_names)    # ['sepal length', ...]
print(iris.target_names)     # ['setosa', 'versicolor', 'virginica']
print(iris.DESCR)            # full description
make_* — Synthetic datasets:

python
from sklearn.datasets import (
    make_classification,
    make_regression,
    make_clustering,
    make_blobs,
    make_circles,
    make_moons,
    make_checkerboard
)

# Classification
X, y = make_classification(
    n_samples=1000,          # number of samples
    n_features=20,           # total features
    n_informative=10,        # useful features
    n_redundant=5,           # redundant features
    n_repeated=0,            # repeated features
    n_classes=3,             # number of classes
    n_clusters_per_class=2,  # clusters per class
    class_sep=0.5,           # class separation
    random_state=42
)

# Regression
X, y = make_regression(
    n_samples=1000,
    n_features=20,
    n_informative=10,
    noise=0.1,               # noise level
    random_state=42
)

# Clustering
X, y = make_blobs(
    n_samples=1000,
    n_features=2,
    centers=3,               # number of clusters
    cluster_std=1.0,         # cluster spread
    random_state=42
)

# Non-linear classification
X, y = make_circles(
    n_samples=1000,
    noise=0.1,
    factor=0.5,              # inner circle size
    random_state=42
)

X, y = make_moons(
    n_samples=1000,
    noise=0.1,
    random_state=42
)
Visual comparison of make_ datasets:*

python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.datasets import (
    make_classification,
    make_blobs,
    make_circles,
    make_moons
)

fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 2, figsize=(12, 10))

datasets = {
    'make_classification': make_classification(
        n_samples=300, n_features=2,
        n_informative=2, n_redundant=0,
        random_state=42
    ),
    'make_blobs': make_blobs(
        n_samples=300, n_features=2,
        centers=3, random_state=42
    ),
    'make_circles': make_circles(
        n_samples=300, noise=0.1,
        random_state=42
    ),
    'make_moons': make_moons(
        n_samples=300, noise=0.1,
        random_state=42
    )
}

colors = ['#2196F3', '#FF9800', '#4CAF50']

for ax, (name, (X, y)) in zip(axes.flat, datasets.items()):
    for class_id in range(len(set(y))):
        mask = y == class_id
        ax.scatter(
            X[mask, 0], X[mask, 1],
            c=colors[class_id],
            label=f'Class {class_id}',
            alpha=0.6
        )
    ax.set_title(name)
    ax.legend()

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('datasets_comparison.png', dpi=100)
plt.show()

What is the best way to create instance data for testing deployment

12:09 PM
Great question! Here are the best approaches for creating test data for a deployed endpoint:

The golden rule:

Test data must have exact same features
as training data — same order, same scale
Option 1 — Use real samples from test set (best):

python
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import json

# Pick samples from your existing test set
test_samples = X_test.iloc[:5]

print("Test samples shape:", test_samples.shape)
print("Feature names:", list(test_samples.columns))
print("\nSample data:")
print(test_samples)

# Convert to format for endpoint
payload = {
    "instances": test_samples.values.tolist()
}

print("\nPayload for endpoint:")
print(json.dumps(payload, indent=2))
Option 2 — Use statistical properties of training data:

python
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd

# Get statistics from training data
stats = X_train.describe()
print(stats)

# Generate samples within realistic ranges
def generate_realistic_sample(X_train, n_samples=1):
    samples = []
    for _ in range(n_samples):
        sample = {}
        for col in X_train.columns:
            mean = X_train[col].mean()
            std  = X_train[col].std()
            # Generate value within 2 standard deviations
            value = np.random.normal(mean, std)
            sample[col] = round(float(value), 4)
        samples.append(sample)
    return pd.DataFrame(samples)

# Generate 5 realistic samples
realistic_samples = generate_realistic_sample(X_train, n_samples=5)
print("\nGenerated realistic samples:")
print(realistic_samples)

# Convert to payload
payload = {
    "instances": realistic_samples.values.tolist()
}
Option 3 — Create edge cases to stress test:

python
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

feature_names = X_train.columns.tolist()

edge_cases = pd.DataFrame([
    # Average sample
    X_train.mean(),

    # All features at maximum
    X_train.max(),

    # All features at minimum
    X_train.min(),

    # High values only
    X_train.mean() + 2 * X_train.std(),

    # Low values only
    X_train.mean() - 2 * X_train.std(),

    # Random sample
    X_train.sample(1, random_state=42).iloc[0]
])

edge_cases.index = [
    'average',
    'all_max',
    'all_min',
    'high_values',
    'low_values',
    'random'
]

print("Edge cases:")
print(edge_cases)

payload = {
    "instances": edge_cases.values.tolist()
}
Option 4 — Save test data to file for reuse:

python
import json
import pandas as pd

# Create comprehensive test set

    "description": "Test instances for xgboost-synthetic-classifier",
    "feature_names": X_train.columns.tolist(),
    "instances": {
        "real_samples": X_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist(),
        "edge_cases": {
            "average":     X_train.mean().values.tolist(),
            "maximum":     X_train.max().values.tolist(),
            "minimum":     X_train.min().values.tolist(),
            "high":        (X_train.mean() + 2*X_train.std()).values.tolist(),
            "low":         (X_train.mean() - 2*X_train.std()).values.tolist()
        },
        "true_labels": y_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()
    }
}

# Save locally
with open('test_data.json', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(test_data, f, indent=2)
print("✓ Saved locally")

# Save to S3 for reuse
s3 = boto3.client('s3', region_name=region)
s3.upload_file(
    'test_data.json',
    bucket,
    'synthetic/test_data/test_data.json'
)
print(f"✓ Saved to S3")

# Load later from S3
response = s3.get_object(
    Bucket=bucket,
    Key='synthetic/test_data/test_data.json'
)
loaded = json.loads(response['Body'].read().decode())
print("\nLoaded test data:")
print(f"  Features:     {len(loaded['feature_names'])}")
print(f"  Real samples: {len(loaded['instances']['real_samples'])}")
print(f"  Edge cases:   {len(loaded['instances']['edge_cases'])}")
Option 5 — Complete test harness:

python
import boto3
import json
import numpy as np
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score

class EndpointTester:
    def __init__(self, endpoint_name, region='us-east-1'):
        self.endpoint_name = endpoint_name
        self.runtime = boto3.client(
            'sagemaker-runtime',
            region_name=region
        )

    def predict(self, instances):
        response = self.runtime.invoke_endpoint(
            EndpointName=self.endpoint_name,
            ContentType='application/json',
            Body=json.dumps({"instances": instances})
        )
        return json.loads(response['Body'].read().decode())

    def test_real_samples(self, X_test, y_test,
                          n_samples=10, class_names=None):
        print("Testing real samples...")
        samples = X_test.iloc[:n_samples].values.tolist()
        true_labels = y_test.iloc[:n_samples].values.tolist()

        result = self.predict(samples)
        predictions = result['predictions']

        print(f"\n{'Sample':<8} {'True':<12} {'Predicted':<12} {'Correct'}")
        print("-" * 45)
        for i, (true, pred) in enumerate(
            zip(true_labels, predictions)
        ):
            if class_names:
                true_label = class_names[int(true)]
                pred_label = class_names[int(pred)]
            else:
                true_label = str(true)
                pred_label = str(pred)
            correct = "✓" if int(true) == int(pred) else "✗"
            print(f"{i+1:<8} {true_label:<12} {pred_label:<12} {correct}")

        accuracy = accuracy_score(
            true_labels,
            [int(p) for p in predictions]
        )
        print(f"\nAccuracy: {accuracy*100:.1f}%")

    def test_edge_cases(self, X_train, class_names=None):
        print("\nTesting edge cases...")
        edge_cases = {
            'average':    X_train.mean().values.tolist(),
            'maximum':    X_train.max().values.tolist(),
            'minimum':    X_train.min().values.tolist(),
            'high':       (X_train.mean() + 2*X_train.std()).values.tolist(),
            'low':        (X_train.mean() - 2*X_train.std()).values.tolist()
        }

        instances = list(edge_cases.values())
        result = self.predict(instances)
        predictions = result['predictions']

        print(f"\n{'Case':<12} {'Predicted Class'}")
        print("-" * 30)
        for case, pred in zip(edge_cases.keys(), predictions):
            if class_names:
                pred_label = class_names[int(pred)]
            else:
                pred_label = str(pred)
            print(f"{case:<12} {pred_label}")

    def run_all_tests(self, X_train, X_test,
                      y_test, class_names=None):
        print("=" * 50)
        print(f"Testing endpoint: {self.endpoint_name}")
        print("=" * 50)
        self.test_real_samples(X_test, y_test,
                               class_names=class_names)
        self.test_edge_cases(X_train,
                             class_names=class_names)
        print("\n✓ All tests complete")


# Use it
tester = EndpointTester('xgboost-synthetic-classifier')
tester.run_all_tests(
    X_train, X_test, y_test,
    class_names=['class_0', 'class_1', 'class_2']
)
Summary — which option to use:

Option	Best for
Real samples	Quick sanity check
Statistical samples	Realistic testing
Edge cases	Stress testing
Save to S3	Reusable test suite
Test harness	Production testing
My recommendation — use Option 5 (test harness). It combines everything, is reusable, and gives you a proper testing framework you can reuse for any future model. Want to try it?

5:20 PM
Two problems:

get_execution_role() only works inside SageMaker — not on your Mac
sagemaker.Session() needs region specified
Fix:

python
from sagemaker.sklearn.model import SKLearnModel
import sagemaker
import boto3

# Fix 1 - specify region explicitly
region = 'us-east-1'
bucket = 'mva-python-code-east'
boto_session = boto3.Session(region_name=region)
session = sagemaker.Session(boto_session=boto_session)

# Fix 2 - get role manually instead of get_execution_role()
account_id = boto3.client('sts', region_name=region).get_caller_identity()['Account']
role = f"arn:aws:iam::{account_id}:role/SageMakerRole"

print(f"Account: {account_id}")
print(f"Role: {role}")
print(f"Model: s3://{bucket}/synthetic/model/model.tar.gz")

# Verify model exists in S3
s3 = boto3.client('s3', region_name=region)
try:
    s3.head_object(Bucket=bucket, Key='synthetic/model/model.tar.gz')
    print("✓ Model found in S3")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"✗ Model NOT found in S3: {e}")
    print("Run the model save and upload cells first!")
Then deploy:

The hang is likely a credentials or network issue. Let's diagnose:

Run each line separately:

python
# Cell 1 - test boto3 basic connection

5:44 PM
Yes all of this is normal! Here's what's happening:

Why deployment takes long (10-20 mins):

Step 1 - Provision EC2 instance     (~3-5 mins)
Step 2 - Pull Docker container      (~3-5 mins)
Step 3 - Load your model            (~1-2 mins)
Step 4 - Health checks              (~2-3 mins)
Step 5 - Endpoint becomes InService (~1-2 mins)
─────────────────────────────────────────────
Total:                               10-20 mins
Check status while waiting:

python
import boto3

sm_client = boto3.client('sagemaker', region_name='us-east-1')

response = sm_client.describe_endpoint(
    EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier'
)

print(f"Status:           {response['EndpointStatus']}")
print(f"Secondary status: {response['FailureReason'] if response['EndpointStatus'] == 'Failed' else 'N/A'}")
Or check from CLI:

bash
aws sagemaker describe-endpoint \
  --endpoint-name xgboost-synthetic-classifier \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --query '[EndpointStatus,FailureReason]' \
  --output table
Status meanings:

Status	Meaning
Creating	Still deploying — be patient
InService	Ready to use!
Failed	Something went wrong
Updating	Being updated
For future deployments — fix the role issue properly:

python
# Add this to your notebook setup cell
# so you never need to hardcode again
import boto3

account_id = boto3.client(
    'sts', region_name='us-east-1'
).get_caller_identity()['Account']

role = f"arn:aws:iam::{account_id}:role/SageMakerRole"
print("Role:", role)

The container failed health check. Let's check CloudWatch logs to find the exact error:

python
import boto3

logs_client = boto3.client('logs', region_name='us-east-1')

log_group = '/aws/sagemaker/Endpoints/xgboost-synthetic-classifier'

# Get log streams
try:
    streams = logs_client.describe_log_streams(
        logGroupName=log_group,
        orderBy='LastEventTime',
        descending=True
    )
    
    print("Log streams found:")
    for stream in streams['logStreams']:
        print(f"  {stream['logStreamName']}")
    
    # Get logs from first stream
    if streams['logStreams']:
        stream_name = streams['logStreams'][0]['logStreamName']
        logs = logs_client.get_log_events(
            logGroupName=log_group,
            logStreamName=stream_name,
            limit=50
        )
        
        print("\nLogs:")
        print("-" * 60)
        for event in logs['events']:
            print(event['message'])
            
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")

7:37 PM
The error is clear — xgboost is not installed in the SageMaker container. Fix by adding a requirements.txt file:

Step 1 — Create requirements.txt in your scripts folder:

python
# Run this cell
with open('scripts/requirements.txt', 'w') as f:
    f.write("xgboost\njoblib\nnumpy\nscikit-learn\n")

print("✓ requirements.txt created")

# Verify
with open('scripts/requirements.txt', 'r') as f:
    print(f.read())
Step 2 — Delete failed endpoint:

python
import boto3

sm_client = boto3.client('sagemaker', region_name='us-east-1')
sm_client.delete_endpoint(
    EndpointName='xgboost-synthetic-classifier'
)
print("✓ Failed endpoint deleted")
Step 3 — Redeploy with requirements.txt:

python
from sagemaker.sklearn.model import SKLearnModel
import sagemaker
import boto3

region = 'us-east-1'
bucket = 'mva-python-code-east'
boto_session = boto3.Session(region_name=region)
session = sagemaker.Session(boto_session=boto_session)

# Your hardcoded role
role = 'arn:aws:iam::YOUR_ACCOUNT_ID:role/SageMakerRole'

sklearn_model = SKLearnModel(
    model_data=f's3://{bucket}/synthetic/model/model.tar.gz',
    role=role,
    entry_point='inference.py',
    source_dir='scripts',      # requirements.txt is here
    framework_version='1.2-1',
    py_version='py3',
    sagemaker_session=session
)

print("Deploying...")
predictor = sklearn_model.deploy(
    initial_instance_count=1,
    instance_type='ml.t2.medium',
    endpoint_name='xgboost-synthetic-classifier-v2'
)

print(f"✓ Endpoint deployed: {predictor.endpoint_name}")
Your scripts/ folder should now look like:

scripts/
├── inference.py      ← inference code
└── requirements.txt  ← xgboost, joblib, numpy, sklearn

10:31 PM
The predictor is sending application/x-npy format but your input_fn only handles application/json. Two ways to fix:

Fix 1 — Simplest: update inference.py to handle both formats:

python
inference_script = '''
import joblib
import numpy as np
import os
import json

def model_fn(model_dir):
    model = joblib.load(os.path.join(model_dir, "model.joblib"))
    return model

def input_fn(request_body, request_content_type):
    """Handle both json and numpy formats"""
    if request_content_type == "application/json":
        data = json.loads(request_body)
        # handle both {"instances": [...]} and direct array
        if isinstance(data, dict):
            return np.array(data["instances"])
        return np.array(data)
    
    elif request_content_type == "application/x-npy":
        import io
        return np.load(io.BytesIO(request_body), allow_pickle=True)
    
    elif request_content_type == "text/csv":
        import io
        return np.loadtxt(io.StringIO(request_body), delimiter=",")
    
    raise ValueError(f"Unsupported content type: {request_content_type}")

def predict_fn(input_data, model):
    predictions = model.predict(input_data)
    probabilities = model.predict_proba(input_data)
    return {
        "predictions": predictions.tolist(),
        "probabilities": probabilities.tolist()
    }

def output_fn(prediction, content_type):
    return json.dumps(prediction)
'''

with open('scripts/inference.py', 'w') as f:
    f.write(inference_script)

print("✓ inference.py updated")
Fix 2 — Simplest of all: tell predictor to use JSON:

python
from sagemaker.predictor import Predictor
from sagemaker.serializers import JSONSerializer
from sagemaker.deserializers import JSONDeserializer

# Recreate predictor with JSON serializer
predictor = Predictor(
    endpoint_name='xgboost-synthetic-classifier-v2',
    sagemaker_session=session,
    serializer=JSONSerializer(),
    deserializer=JSONDeserializer()
)

# Now call with dict directly
test_samples = X_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()
result = predictor.predict({"instances": test_samples})

print("Predictions:", result)
Fix 2 is quickest — no need to redeploy! Just recreate the predictor with the right serializer.

It's working! Let's make the output more readable:

python
import json

class_names = ['class_0', 'class_1', 'class_2']
true_labels = y_test.iloc[:5].values.tolist()

predictions = result['predictions']
probabilities = result['probabilities']

print("Results from AWS Endpoint:")
print("=" * 65)
print(f"{'Sample':<8} {'True':<12} {'Predicted':<12} {'Confidence':<12} {'Correct'}")
print("-" * 65)

for i, (true, pred, probs) in enumerate(zip(
    true_labels, predictions, probabilities
)):
    true_label = class_names[int(true)]
    pred_label = class_names[int(pred)]
    confidence = max(probs) * 100
    correct = "✓" if int(true) == int(pred) else "✗"
    print(f"{i+1:<8} {true_label:<12} {pred_label:<12} {confidence:<11.1f}% {correct}")

print("-" * 65)
correct_count = sum(1 for t, p in zip(true_labels, predictions) if int(t) == int(p))
print(f"Accuracy: {correct_count}/5 = {correct_count/5*100:.1f}%")

print("\nProbability breakdown:")
print("-" * 65)
for i, (pred, probs) in enumerate(zip(predictions, probabilities)):
    print(f"\nSample {i+1} → {class_names[int(pred)]}")
    for cls, prob in zip(class_names, probs):
        bar = "█" * int(prob * 30)
        print(f"  {cls}: {prob*100:5.1f}% {bar}")
